2.13 Not only this percentage of utilization in respect of States/ UTs like A&N Islands, 
Haryana, J&K, Lakshadweep, Meghalaya and Uttrakhand was between 45% to 50%. The 
very fact that the level of expenditure of Rs. 3254.29 crore as on 31.03.2009 is even less 
than the opening balance of Rs. 4150.07 crore as on 01.04.2008 available with States, 
compels the Committee to conclude that States are simply not able to take the full benefit 
of Central funds. A number of reasons ha ve been cited by the Department being 
responsible for low capacity of States.  The Committee is not prepared to accept the 
Department’s contention that delay in ta king up execution of civil works due to 
State healthcare departments being dependent on PWD and other State agencies, as 
the main factor behind such a scenario . The other reasons like requirement of 
opening of Back Account at each level and lack of proper planning for under-served 
areas are also equally unconvincing. The Co mmittee strongly feels that these are all 
such matters which should have been reso lved in the initial one or two years of 
NRHM. The Committee would also like to point out that monitoring and joint 
review missions of NRHM need to be followed by resolving of major constraints like 

 81
shortage of qualified and trained manpower and lack of infrastructural facilities. 
Otherwise allocated funds will contin ue to remain under-utilized every year 
defeating the very purpose of launching of NRHM in a mission mode.       
 
2.14 The Committee is surprised to note that under RCH Flexible Pool also 
Opening Balance amounting to Rs. 1157.46 crore was available as on 01.04.2008 
with different States. Assam with availability of Rs. 126.67 crore, Maharashtra with 
Rs. 209.69 crore, Tamil Nady with Rs. 97.73 crore, U.P with Rs. 192.33 crore and 
West Bengal with Rs. 101.59 crore can be considered slow perf orming States. Not 
only this, States like Goa, Meghalaya and Tr ipura failed to utilise even the opening 
balance during the course of the year. Perc entage of utilization of total allocated 
funds (opening balance + Release) was very low in respect of A&N Islands (44%), 
Assam (53%), Goa(23%), Haryana (52%), Himachal Pradesh (53%), J&K (40%), 
Manipur (54%), Meghalaya  (26%), Nagaland (45%), Tamil Nadu (40%), Tripura 
(33%) and West Bengal (40%). The Committee is very disappointed with such a 
utilization status, specially because under RCH Flexible Pool, States/ UTs have the 
flexibility to plan on a need basis without being bound by rigid normative 
prescriptions. The Committee, therefore, feel s that with more than two-third of the 
mission period already being over, it is high  time that the Department identifies the 
problem areas in the implementation of var ious components of RCH Flexible Pool 
and takes measures for resolving the same. A beginning can be made by 
approaching the states lagging behind on a priority basis. 
 
III INFRASTUCTURE STRENGTHENING 
 
Healthcare Units  
 
3.1 NRHM aims at strengthening of Primary Health Infrastructure and improving 
effective service delivery across the country. Sub-centers, Primary Healthcare Centers 
and Community Health Centres are the three cornerstones on which rural infrastructure is 
built upon. The Secretary, during the course of deposition before the Committee on 6 th 
April, 2010, highlighted the overall progress ma de in the achievement level of different 
healthcare infrastructure of NRHM, stating that as on date 1,46,036 Health Sub Centers  
(HSCs) were functional with 9144 new HS Cs taken up for construction and 8997 for 
renovation; 23,458 PHCs were functional with 8324 PHCs  functioning 24X7 while 1009 
new PHCs have been taken up for construction and 2081 for renovation. Out of 4276 

 82
CHCs– 930 were added after NRHM. Among the District Hospitals (DHs), 578 District 
Hospitals were functional with 57 new DHs  being constructed and 357 DHs have been 
taken up for renovation. Besides, 2463 centres were functioning as First Referral Units.  
 
3.2 The Committee while taking note of progress made in strengthening the 
primary health infrastructure after the la unch of NRHM would like to point out 
that considerable gap still continues to pe rsist. In this connection, Committee’s 
attention has been drawn by Performance Audit of NRHM by CAG (Report No. 8 
of 2009-10) there was a shortfall of 43987 Sub-Centres (27%) in 22 States/ UTs, 8613 
PHCs (31%) in 21 States/ UTs and 4200 CHCs (55%) in 23 States./ UTs which were 
required to be created during the NRHM period (2005-12). Target of creation of 
30% of new infrastructure by 2007, i.e. 13196 sub-centres, 2585 PHCs and 1261 
CHCs could not be fulfilled. According to the CAG Report, as many as 14 States/ 
UTs failed to take any initiative for bridging the gap. Out of the target of 43987 sub-
centres 8613 PHCs and 4200 CHCs only 2562 Sub-centres, 685 PHCs and 138 CHCs 
could be set-up. The Committee only hopes that 935 Sub-Centres, 178 PHCs and 67 
CHCs reported to be under construction during CAG Audit must have been 
completed and become fully functional by  now. The Committee would like to point 
out that even as per the target of 2007, there was a shortfall of 9749 sub-centres, also 
1722 PHCs and 1056 CHCs. Against this backdrop, bridging the gap of 43987 sub-
centres, 8613 PHCs and 4200 CHCs by the end of mission period i.e. 2012 seems to 
be an uphill task. The Committee would like to be point out that the targets set up 
under NRHM were as per the population norms of 2001. The shortfall of 
infrastructure is bound to increase manifold once 2011 census is over and 
population norms revise. The Committee has also taken note of the fact that only 
very few states like Assam, Chhatisgarh,  Rajasthan, Uttarkhand and Lakshadweep 
had made an attempt to achieve the target of 2007, while the remaining states 
reported nil achievement. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the 
Department has to pursue vigorously with all the non-performing states, otherwise 
even the basic objective of NRHM of havi ng the minimum required infrastructure 
would also remain unfulfilled.  
 
3.3 On a query about the progress in the construction work of sub-centres under the 
‘Infrastructure Strengthening Project’of NRHM, the Committee was informed that the 

 83
progress of Sub-Centre construction is being followed on priority basis to ensure 
functional facilities at Sub-Centre level across the country at the earliest. As per RHS-
2008, out of total 1,46,036 Sub-Centres functioning across the country, a total of 78,803 
were functioning in Government buildings, and  9684 SCs were being constructed across 
the country. The Committee was given to under stand that estimated cost of construction 
is Rs.968.4 crores at the rate of Rs.10 lakhs for 9684 sub-centres, for which funds were 
released through NRHM Flexible Pool.  
3.4 The Committee is perturbed to find that out of 1,46,036 Sub-Centres, as many as 
67233 Centre are functioning from rented bu ildings. From the details furnished by 
the Department, the Committee finds that a large number of States have simply 
failed to take benefit from Central funds being provided for construction/ 
upgradation of sub-centre buildings. In St ates/UTs like Himachal Pradesh, J&K, 
Tamil Nadu, A&N Islands, D&N Haveli, Daman & Diu, Chandigarh, Lakshadweep, 
Puducherry no construction activity has been  initated so far. In States which have 
taken some initiative in this regard, per centage of sub-centres being strengthened/ 
upgraded is very low.  
3.5 The Committee is not convinced by the clarification given by the Department 
that in some States, sub-cen tres are working in other Go vernment buildings/ spaces 
provided by Panchayats etc. The Committee also apprehends that condition of PHC 
and CHC buildings would also be no better. The fact remains that there is an urgent 
need for undertaking a review of present condition of all the Sub-centre/ PHC/ CHC 
buildings across the country and undertak e the required construction under a time-
bound Action Plan.  
 
3.6 The Committee’s attention is also drawn by the damaging report given by 
the CAG in its latest report on this cr ucial aspect. As per CAG report, among 
audited units, 216 sub-centres (16%) of 10 States and 19 PHCs (3% of 4 states, were 
operating without buildings 435 sub-centres (32%) of 28 states/ UTs and 102 PHCs 
(15%) of 17 States and 7 CHCs of 4 Stat es were operating in a rented building/ 
Panchayat Bhawan/ Others. 217 Sub-cent res (16%) of 16 States/ UTs, 86 PHCs 
(13%) of 16 States/ UTs and 23 CHCs (7%) of 5 States/ UTs were functioning in 
dilapidated buildings.  
 

 84

infrastructural facilities noticed by the test checks carried out by the CAG. 
Following details are self-revealing:-

The Committee would like to point out that  Performance Audit by CAG covered all 
the 33 States/ UTs with 123 districts cove ring 2369 health Centres.  These 123 
districts were selected from all the States. In each selected district-3 CHCs, 6 PHCs 
and 12 Sub-Centres were audited. The Committee, taking note of CAG findings, in 
these 123 districts, apprehends that position  would be more or less the same in the 
remaining districts of the country. 
 
3.8 The Committee would like to point out that one of the key objectives of 
NRHM is to provide improved health care services through creation of adequate 
number of SCs, PHCs and CHCs. However, this basic goal of NRHM has failed to 
show the desired results. This has been  amply proved in the latest Performance 
Audit of NRHM conducted by the CAG. The Committee has also been expressing 
grave concern regarding lack of adequate rural health infrastructure, especially in 
High Focus States from time to time. Even though NRHM has completed its 5
th year 
since commencement, much remains to be accomplished so far as infrastructure 
development is concerned.  With two more years of the Mission period remaining, 
the Committee wonders if the targets to overcome the shortfall of Health Care 
Infrastructure would be met in the real sense. The Committee, therefore, once again 

 85
urges the Department to quicken the pace of  implementation so that it does not 
remain very far off from the targeted goals.

light on the latest position of sanctioned pos t of ANMs for Sub-Centres and PHCs, the 
Department has informed that as per available latest data, there are a total number of 
1,87,902 ANMs (1,39,798 in 2005) at Sub Centr es/PHCs presently. From a detailed 
analysis of the information furnished by the Department the Committee notes that  in the 
following States there is a gap between sanctioned posts and in-position posts

ANMs have been recruited on contract basis by the States across the country.  The 
Committee would like to point out that the filling of vacancy of ANMs at Sub-Centres 
and PHCs is a thrust area under NRHM.  However, the clarification given by the 
Department that limited capacity at ANM schools poses problem in availability of ANMs 
depicts another glaring shortcoming in the provision of healthcare facilities in rural areas.

The Committee is not ready to accept the contention of the Department that since the 
training of ANM requires 18 months, it will take  some time in availability of ANMs and 
filling up of the vacant ANM posts at various levels.  The fact remains that the key 
functionary, i.e. ANM, is not there at all the health centres indicates that large number of 
targeted beneficiaries still continue to be deprived of the very basic healthcare facilities.

Lab-Technicians at PHCs and CHCs 
3.15 Out of the required number of 27,734 of Lab-Technicians, only 15,223 posts have

being Kerala, Himachal Prasdesh, Uttarkhand and Tamil Nadu and Punjab. The 
clarification for non-availability of required technical manpower given by the 
Department is that under NRHM, Lab-Technicians are permitted to be engaged on 

 88
contract basis. Moreover, the multi-skilling of Lab-Technicians is also encouraged to 
provide services for various purposes at CHC or PHCs wherever single Lab-Technician 
is available. To the Committee’s observation that generally there is a mismatch between 
the required and sanctioned number of posts of different workers of Sub-Centres, the 
Department has stated that many of the States are yet to sanction the required number of 
posts of Lab-Technicians; hence, a mis-match between number of posts required and 
sanctioned by the States is observed.

where vacancies of doctors are highest are A ssam, Bihar, Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, 
Punjab, U.P. and West Bengal. The Committee observes that as on 31
st January, 2010, a 
total of 8624 doctors have been recruited at PHCs on contract basis.

finds that against the required number of 17104 Specialist for CHCs, sanctioned strength 
is less than half i.e. 8376 and Specialists in-position are only 4279. State-specific details 
are more disappointing. While no specialists have been posted in CHCs in Manipur, 
Mizoram, Delhi, Lakshadweep and Puducherry, no information is available in this regard 
with respect of Orissa, Tamil Nadu and Tripura. The Committee is also constrained to 
observe that Specialists recruited by the States fall short of their actual requirements.

3.18 The Committee is also quite disappointed with the progress made over the 
past four years regarding filling up the vacant posts of the various healthcare 
functionaries under NRHM. The Committe e observes that one of the most 
important objective with which the Mission was launched at the outset was to 
ensure the availability of the various healthcare functionaries, i.e. ANMs/ MPWs(M) 
/LHVs/ Lab Technicians, doctors and speci alists at Sub-Centres, PHCs and CHCs 
at the right places.  Realizing that thei r presence is essential for the smooth 
functioning of health centres and hence, important for the success of the Mission, 
the Committee has been recommending to the Department year after year to take 
up the issue of non-filling up of the vaca ncies with the States/UTs. However, there 
seems to be no significant improvement in the situation.  The Committee feels that 
four years of focused attention, and cons iderable infusion of funds under NRHM, 
aimed to create a brigade of healthcare is a long enough time for the situation to 
have improved. Neither the flow of funds  nor the efforts of the Department are 
found wanting. The only conclusion the Committee can draw is that required 
manpower for the Health Centres is simply not available in majority of the States. 
 
3.19 The Committee also notes with dismay that the latest data about healthcare 
functionaries is not available with the Department.  The Committee would like to 
emphasize  that building up of capacity at the grass roots level is an essential part of 
a proper planning process and improvements in this regard can only be noticed if 
the planned measures are put to implementation. One must not forget that these key 
functionaries are also pivotal in the collec tion and dissemination of health related 
information from the field levels. The Comm ittee would, therefore, appreciate if 
updated data is got collected by the Depa rtment in coordination with the States/ 
UTs. Only then it would be possible to  make a proper assessment of the ground 
realities and plan for augmentation of both the extant and future capacity of 
manpower in the States.  
 

 90
3.20 Another area of concern for the Committee is the number of contractual 
appointments being made under the Mission to fill up the various vacant posts. The 
Committee observes that out of total 1,53,568 ANMs in-position in health centres 
across the country, 46,315 ANMs are on contract basis. The Committee is 
constrained to note that such ANMS are co ncentrated in a few states as indicated 
below:-

Similarly, out of 4279 Specialists in-position, as many as 2426 are on contract basis.  
3.21 The Committee feels that for effective delivery of public health services, it is 
essential that service personnel are present continuously in their place of postings. 
However, with more and more number of  contractual manpower being recruited 
under NRHM, the Committee fears that the accountability mechanism under the 
programme would get diluted, thereby affect ing the delivery of quality services to 
the masses. The Committee, accordingly, recommends to the Department that an 
All India level drive for filling up of the vacant posts, first against the sanctioned 
strength and thereafter against the required strength needs to be  undertaken in a 
mission mode so that by the end of NRHM significant improvement is more than 
evident. 
 
IV       MISSION FLEXIBLE POOL 
 
4.1 Mission Flexible Pool provides for act ivities like selection and training of 
ASHAs, upgradation of CHCs and PHCs to First Referral Units and Indian Public Health 
Standards (IPHS), constitution of Rogi Kalyan Samitis and District Hospital Management 
Committees, Mobile Medical Units, untied funds  for Sub-Centres etc. States have the 
flexibility of utilizing the funds as per thei r priorities for the approved activities. The 

 91
Committee notes that maximum expenditure (31%) is towards infrastructure upgradation, 
followed by Untied Funds (24%) and State Innovations (9%). 
 
4.2 The Committee has been informed that the total approved outlay under Mission 
Flexible Pool for the Eleventh Five Year Plan is Rs. 33181.07 crore and the expenditure 
figures for 2007-08,   2008-09 and 2009-10 (upto January 2010) are Rs. 3151.64 crore, 
Rs. 2599.13 crore and Rs. 2479.17 crore respectively, totaling Rs. 8229.94 crore for the 
three years taken together.  Thus an amount of Rs. 24951.13 crore (approx.) is still left to 
be utilized during the remaining two financial years of the Eleventh Plan. However, 
against the projected demand of Rs. 8000.00 cr ore, plan allocation of Rs. 3569.70 crore, 
has been made in 2010-11. 
 
4.3 The Committee observes that allocation of Rs. 3567.27 crore for 2009-10 was 
reduced to Rs. 3276.17 crore at the RE stage. This cut was imposed by the Ministry of 
Finance on account of slow pace of expenditu re. The Committee concerned to note that 
as on 31
st January, 2010, expenditure reported was only Rs. 2479.17 crore. Position was 
no better during 2008-09 with  States like U.P, Orissa, Madhya Pradesh, Himachal 
Pradesh could utilize less than 50% while Bihar and Chattisgarh were able to spend only 
13% and 17% respectively. Very low use of untied funds provided under the Mission 
Flexible Pool in Bihar and delayed transfer of  funds to the districts in Chattisgarh have 
been cited as the main reason for unsatisfactory performance in the two States.  
 
4.4 The Committee is anguished to note the huge shortfall in utilization of the 
budgeted funds and also the substantial variation between the projected demand for 
funds and the sanctioned provisions.  The Committee observes that at a time when 
equitable access to health facilities across the social and geographical expanse of the 
country is the declared policy of the Govern ment such inefficiencies in utilization of 
funds can prove to be a potent deterrent. The Committee would like the Department 
to impress upon all concerned including the State Governments the imperative need 
to review their existing mechanism for assessment of requirement of funds and take 
corrective action to overcome the deficiencies in the existing system so that the 
possibility of surrendering unspent/under-utilized funds is obviated.

services. Similarly, as on date only 2463 PHCs and CHCs have been upgraded as First

achievement level under the most vital component of strengthening of healthcare 
facilities for the rural population as indicated below:-

The Committee observes that the status of PHCs running 24x7 in some of the States 
need to be revisited. States indicated above  need to double up their efforts so as to

for the purpose. The Committee recommends the Department to take up the matter 
of increasing the number of PHCs operating  24x7 as well as upgradation of PHCs/ 
CHCs to FRUs with all the States/ UTs so that a critical service like Health Care is 
available to the targeted beneficiaries wherever required.  
 

 93
4.7 Another initiative under NRHM was to upgrade all CHCs to IPHS standards 
by 2010 in a phased manner in all the States/UTs. From the latest information 
furnished to it the Committee finds that in States/ UTs like Bihar, Chattisgarh, 
Himachal Pradesh, Jharkhand, Sikkim, Andhra Pradesh, Chandigarh, Dadra & 
Nagar Haveli, Lakshawdeep, Delhi, inspite of CHCs being selected for upgradation 
to IPHS, no progress has been reported so far.  
 
4.8 The Committee also notes that the status of percentage of physical 
upgradation of CHCs in States like J&K (26%), Orissa (13%), MP (4%), Tripura 
(33%), Kerala (29%), West Bengal (9 %) and A&N Island (25%) is far from 
satisfactory. The Committee fails to understand as to what could be the inherent 
constraints being faced by these States in upgrading their CHCs inspite of required 
funds being made available by the Centre. The Committee would like to impress 
upon the Department that the very objective of NRHM was to provide quality 
health care facilities which were not available earlier and therefore, the Department 
had embarked upon upgrading the facilities into IPHS. With such low level of 
achievements it cannot be said that the St ates are doing well in the progress of the 
work. This is all the more disturbing wh en there is a considerable gap between the 
required and existing CHCs. The Committee is constrained to note that the 
Department is perhaps not persuing the ma tter with the State Government for the 
need to focus their attention so that the targets are met within their prescribed time-
lines. With only 1255 CHCs taken up for renovation so far, the target of upgrading 
all CHCs to IPHS standards by 2010 is likely to remain unaccomplished.  
 
4.9 As per the mandate of Implementation Framework of NRHM, all Integrated 
District Health Action Plans (IDHAP) were to be finalised by the end of 2008.  On being 
asked regarding the latest State-wise progress made so far, the Department has informed 
that though there was a delay initially, now States/UTs have shown remarkable 
improvement for preparation of IDHAP. As on date, 617 districts of the country have 
already finalised IDHAPs, and teams from NHS RC were sent to backward districts to 
facilitate decentralised planning.  
 
4.10 The Committee, however, notes that  only 22 States/UTs have furnished 
IDHAPs fully, while, even by the end of March 2010, as many as 12 States/UTs are 

 94
yet to furnish the IDHAPs 100 percent. Goa, D&N Haveli and Lakshadweep have 
not been able to submit a single IDHAP, while Rajasthan has been able to furnish 
only 26 out of the 33 IDHAPs. This is the position when timeline to complete the 
submission of IDHAPs by the States/UTs was by the end of 2008. The Committee 
fails to understand what could be the possible reasons for such inordinate delays in 
the submission of IDHAPs.  The Committee would like to emphasise that Integrated 
District Health Action Plans are the very basis for improving the availability of 
health care facilities in a district.  In the absence of a concrete plan of action drawn 
by the districts, proper health care facilit ies for people residing in such districts 
cannot be developed optimally.  The Committee therefore, impresses upon the 
Department to vigorously pursue this issue with concerned States/UTs to furnish the 
same as early as possible. 
 
Rogi Kalyan Samitis 
4.11 As per the latest information made av ailable by the Department, 568 District 
Hospitals in the country have Rogi Kalyan Samitis (RKS). RKS are also there in 4164 
CHCs and 16687 PHCs. Resources are provided to all RKS. 
Committee’s attention has been drawn by the assessment of RKS by the CAG in 
its latest performance Audit of NRHM. A number of discrepancies have been pointed out 
by CAG in its Report. 
• RKS did not receive all the annual Central grants in any State. 
• During 2006-07 in 11 States, Rs. 41 crore were released in excess of 
requirements. 
• In Uttar Pradesh, Rs. 36.60 crore were rele ased to all the 3660 PHCs as corpus 
grant as against only 560 eligible PHCs. 
• In Bihar, State Government released annual grant for 84 Referral Hospitals and 
470 PHCs, inspite of RKS being formed only at 44 Referral Hospitals and 311 
PHCs. 
• Utilisation of funds available with RKS was very low with all 16 States/ UTs 
reporting 31 to 98% available funds with RKS remaining unspent. 
 
4.12 The Committee would emphasis that th ere is an urgent need for monitoring 
of the functioning of RKS at ground level. Merely obtaining quarterly/ annual 

 95
reports about funds allocated/ utilized will not serve the purpose. A beginning needs 
to be made by taking up follow-up action with all the States/ UTs where 
performance of RKS has not been found satisfactory by CAG. 
 
Grants to State Training Institutions 
  
4.13 The success of Family Welfare Programme depends to a large extent, upon the 
availability of qualified, trained and dedi cated healthcare functionaries. Training at 
various level is imparted through the network of Training Centres and Schools. Approved 
outlay of Rs. 669.58 crore has been kept under Eleventh Plan for grants to State Training 
Institutions. The Committee notes that out of this allocation, major funding of Rs. 
520.48 crore is meant for basic training for ANMs/ LHVs and Rs. 56.09 crore for 
basic training for MPWs (Male). The Comm ittee notes that out of Rs. 520.48 crore 
kept for training of ANMs/ LHVs, only Rs . 218.04 crore have been released during 
the first three years of the Eleventh Plan. With Rs. 85.18 crore proposed for 2010-
11, more than Rs. 200.00 crore would be left for the last year of the plan period. Not 
only this, during 2008-09 and 2009-10, th e allocated funds have remained under-
utilised. The Committee finds that funds meant for basic training for MPWs (Male) 
also could not be put to optimum use during the Eleventh Plan period so far. 
4.14 While trying to analyse the reasons for such a state of affairs under the most 
crucial component i.e., training of key functionaries positioned at Sub-Centres and PHCs, 
the Committee finds that limited capacity of ANM schools poses problems in availability 
of ANMs. In other words, the lack of training school for ANMs is also indirectly 
responsible for shortage of ANMs. The Committee was given to understand that States 
have the option to use NRHM resources to restart/ open new ANM training schools. The 
Committee also notes that Lady Health Visitors, a promotional post for ANMs, are 
required to undergo 6 months training in LHV training schools. However, due to LHV 
training schools being closed down in many States, required training was not being 
imparted to LHVs. Situation is more grim w ith respect to training of MPWs (Male) as 
States have simply stopped recruitment of MPWs (Male) for many years due to closing 
down of training schools. The Committee is not aware about the reasons for lack of 
interest on the part of State Governments for creating/ strengthening the training 
schools meant for key Health functionaries with the support of Central Funds. The 
Committee strongly feels that it is high  time for the Department to make an 

 96
assessment of this crucial area in consultation with all the States/ UTs and bring 
about all round improvement. One must not forget that a fully trained and qualified 
health worker alone can handle his job responsibilities effectively. 
 
ASHA  
 
4.15 ASHA - a social health activist - is one of the most important strategic 
intervention under the National Rural Health Mission who is required to inform, interact, 
mobilize and facilitate improved access to preventive and promotive healthcare and also 
provide basic curative care through her drug kits.  The requirement for ASHA is one per 
thousand population with relaxation to this norm depending upon the workload and 
terrain.  ASHA is necessarily a woman of the village between the age group of 25 to 45 
years, who would be selected by the Gram Sabha following an intense community 
mobilization process.   
 
Training of ASHAs 
 
4.16 Capacity building of ASHA is critical in enhancing her effectiveness as a link 
between the community and the health facility.  The initial orientation training is for a 
period of 23 days spread in 5 rounds over a period of 12 months and followed by periodic 
re-training for about 2 days once every alternate month, apart from the on-the-job 
training she would be getting.  Training modu les for this purpose have been developed 
based on a thematic approach and States have also constituted district and block training 
teams for this purpose. The Secretary, during the course of her presentation had informed 
the Committee that till date 7.90 lakhs ASHAs have been selected, and from amongst 
them 75% have been trained upto the IV
th Module and have been provided with Drugs 
kits.  However, as per the details made available to the Committee, out of 7,91,069 
ASHAs selected across the country, only 5,92, 395 ASHAs have been provided training 
upto IVth Module. Considerable gap is found in some of the States, as indicated below: 
 
State Selected ASHAs Training

 4.17 About the State-specific variations in the training of ASHAs, the Committee was 
informed that some State Governments, specially non-high focus States like Tamil Nadu, 
Punjab and West Bengal, had taken up the pr ovision of ASHAs at a later date and hence 
the selection process and training was somewh at delayed. All these States have been 
asked to expedite the training of ASHAs up to the 4 th module and complete the same 
during the current financial year.  
4.18 The Committee views with serious conc ern the gap in meeting the targets of 
ASHA training upto IV
th Module in most of the States. The Committee is not 
convinced by the reason furnished by the Department that the delay is only confined 
to the 4th Module training in ASHAs in some of  the States. Keeping in view the key 
role ASHA plays in achievement of various NRHM objectives, these gaps need to be 
addressed with a sense of urgency. The Committee observes that given the low level 
of availability of health care services in th e rural areas of high focused States, they 
cannot afford to be off-target in such a crucial area as training to ASHA. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends to the Department to impress upon all the 
States/UTs, particularly those lagging behind, to take immediate corrective steps to 
meet the targets fixed in this regard. The Committee would like to be apprised of 
the state-wise updated status of progress made towards bridging the gaps in the        
IV
th Module training of ASHA.

with a large number of ASHAs inspite of being eligible for  drug kits remaining to be 
covered so far. Following details are quite alarming:

provided with drug kits, the initial health activities/ work bestowed upon her would 
be hampered. The Committee would like the Department to furnish state-wise 
updated details of drug kits distribute d to the ASHAs. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that along with all the selected ASHAs being fully trained, equipping 
them with drug kits also needs to be monitored. Gaps, wherever noticed, have to be 
removed without any further delay. At the same time the Department should take 
up with all the States where discrepancies have been noticed in the distribution of 
drug kits.  
 
Role of ASHA 
 
4.22 The Committee notes that the major job responsibilities  of ASHA are :- 
-           awareness generation on  Health, RCH, HIV/AIDS among the people. 
-           advising newly married couples for their special needs. 
-        providing escort services to and guiding pregnant women for availing of 
institutional facilities for Anti-  Natal, Natal and Post-Natal needs. 
-          counselling on nutrition, pregnancy, breast-feeding, immunization and 
family planning 
-          stablishing link with PHCs, private providers and others for service 
requirement of beneficiaries. 
-           mobilising parents for child survival interventions 
-           promoting basic sanitization 

 99
-           provision of non-clinical spacing contraceptives 
-           surveillance of communicable diseases. 
 
4.23 Apart from all these, ASHA is also the primary interface conferred with the duty 
of detection, follow up and counseling of pa tients for all National Health Programmes 
run under the banner of NRHM. She is the Depot Holder for a number of health 
commodities like Vitamin A, Impregnated bed nets, ORS etc. She is also supposed to 
handle treatment of minor ailments and use the Drug Kit assigned to her effectively for 
preliminary investigations.  Another major task  assigned to ASHA is to provide escort 
services for women seeking de livery services at institutions, requiring emergency care 
and MTP services, women and men for clinical contraception services and VCTC. The 
Committee would like to note that it had raised serious apprehensions regarding the 
effectivity of ASHA as a multipurpose social health activist as envisaged under the 
NRHM, given the increasing number of job responsibilities that she was being burdended 
with, in its 27th Report on Demands for Grants 2008-09. The Committee had also voiced 
its concerns on the remuneration package of the ASHA vis a vis the various activities she 
was to be associated with, and had accordingly recommended that the role of ASHA 
needs to be reassessed and reformulated. 
 
4.24  The Committee is aware that the National Health System Resource Centre 
(NHSRC) had been entrusted with the work of reviewing the ‘job responsibilities’ and 
‘Role clarity of ASHA’ as well as that of AWW and ANM. On being asked about the 
latest status on the matter and to furnish details thereof, the Department has informed that 
NHSRC has reviewed the job responsibilities and role clarity of ASHA. To ensure that 
the current roles played by ASHA in various States are reflected in redefining the role 
clarity and job responsibilities, a process of consultations and reviews was undertaken. 
These include Consultations with ASHA Monitoring Group (AMG) members, reviewing 
the reports of the Common Review Mission, decision of various consultative meetings, 
and reports of Phase I evaluation of ASHA programme done in eight States namely 
Assam, Andhra Pradesh, Kerala, Jharkhand, Ra jasthan, Bihar, Orissa and West Bengal. 
Based on these consultations and reviews, a note on the role of ASHA, her 
responsibilities and payment mechanisms  has been prepared by NHSRC.  The 
Department has further stated that NHSRC is in final stage of preparing its Report on 

 100
review of job responsibilities and role clarity of ANM and AWW which would be 
available by March 2010. 
 
4.25 The Committee notes that from the in terim findings of the National Health 
System Resource Centre (NHSRC) note made available to the Committee that 
NHSRC has identified five areas for strengthening the ASHA programme:- 
 
 
- Role Clarity 
- Payment mechanism 
- Training strategies and focus 
- Supervision and Support 
- Financing package. 
 
The initial findings of the NHSRC note, among other recommendations, confirm the 
Committee’s apprehensions regarding role clarity of ASHA and reinforces the 
Committee’s view point that ‘a clear set of measurable health outcomes on priority 
goals’  may be determined for ASHAs with  a ‘provision of a limited range of 
services’. The Committee understands that by now the NHSRC Report on the 
review of job responsibilities and role cl arity of ANM and AWW would have been 
submitted to the Department. The Commi ttee recommends to the Department to 
take due cognizance of the observations of  NHSRC and to take immediate measures 
to implement the same right from the first quarter of 2010-11 so that positive results 
in this regards become visible by the year-end. The Committee also recommends 
that in the meanwhile, a minimum amount per month as allowance as 
recommended by the Mission Steering Group, apart from the incentives per 
programme as currently done, may be given to ASHA as remuneration.  
 
Mobile Medical Units

door-step of the needy people. Operationalisation of Mobile Medical Units (MMUs) in 
400 districts in the country by March 2009 was the target set under NRHM. As per the 
latest details made available to the Committee there are 354 Districts where facility for 
MMUs under NRHM is available. The Committee has also been given to understand that 
many States have made their own arrange ment for MMUs. 1263 State MMUs fall under 

 101
this category. From the State-specific information given by the Department, the 
Committee is constrained to conclude that this very crucial component is simply not 
visible in some of the States as indicated below:

4.27 The Committee has been given to understa nd that States were advised to analyse 
this situation vis-à-vis the existing resources available locally/ on the spot. Accordingly, 
several States have asked for financial assist ance for MMUs and accordingly, funds were 
released to the States. However, the Commi ttee doubts whether funds so released were 
put to any use. Committee’s apprehensions have been confirmed by the latest CAG 
findings in Performance Audit of NRHM. As per CAG, during 2006-07, Rs. 19.95 crore 
and Rs. 5.13 crore were released to U.P. (for 70 Districts) and to Punjab (for 18 Districts) 
respectively which included excess release of Rs. 2.28 crore (U.P. ) and Rs. 58.50 lakhs 
(Punjab). During 2007-08, Rs. 12.56 crore was released to 5 States i.e. Karnataka,

operational in any district of as many as 13 States, and in majority of districts in the 
remaining 22 States MMUs were found non-functional.  
 
4.29 The Committee is not very convinced by the Department’s replies regarding 
procurement and provision of MMUs. The Department has informed that States, 
based on their need and the resources pu t forth their demands for MMUs in the 
state PIPs. However, the Committee feels th at the Department should take active 
interest in promoting and providing MMUs to the uncovered/ under-covered 
districts of the country. The Committee  notes that States/ UTs like U.P, 
Chhattisgarh, Himachal Pradesh are some of the States where the health care 
indices are already on slippery ground. However, with not even a single MMU 

 102
available in any of the district in the afo resaid States one can imagine the status of 
accessibility of healthcare services in under-served/ unserved areas.                        
Non-operationalisation/ inadequate functioning of MMUs aggravated the problem 
further. The Committee observ es that the Department would have to devise other 
means and methods beyond mere pursuance with the States, to impress the need for 
the presence of MMUs at the district level so that some emergency medical services 
can be made available to needy. 
 
4.30 Of the 354 MMUs operational in the country, the fact as to how many of 
them are actually functional is unclear. Mo reover, it is also unclear as to whether 
any assessment has been made to find out that the MMUs purchased till date 
confirm to the standards. CAG findings in its latest report are quite alarming. The 
Committee notes that MMUs procured in Manipur remained unutilized as of 
October, 2008 for want of technical know-how and Specialist doctors. Similarly, 
MMUs were duly procured by Tripura but remained unutilized. Expression of 
interest was floated in May, 2008 in viting interested NGOs/ Charitable 
Organisations for operationalising the MMUs. 
 
4.31 The Committee can well imagine that there may be any number of similar 
cases in other States also. In such a scen ario, the Committee strongly feels that the 
Ministry needs to undertake a country-w ide review about functional status of 
MMUs. Thereafter, constant monitoring at regular intervals has to be carried out so 
as to ensure the accessibility of healthcare facilities to the general masses 
 
  
V. RCH FLEXIBLE POOL 
 
5.1 The main objective of RCH Flexible Pool is to bring about the change in the three 
critical health indicators: Infant Mortality Rate (IMR), Maternal Mortality Ratio (MMR) 
and Total Fertility Rate (TFR) consistent with the goal of National Population Policy 
2002, the Tenth and Eleventh Plans and Millennium Development Goals (MDGs). The 
targets to be achieved by the year 2012 are to  bring down IMR to less than 30, MMR to 
less than 100 and TFR to 2.1. Against this, the achievement levels as per latest available 
studies are: IMR 53 (SRS 2008), MMR 254 (SRS 2004-06) & TFR 2.6 (SRS 2008). One 
of the most important strategies adopted under the Programme is the freedom given to the 

 103
States to prepare their Annual State and District level Project Implementation Plan (PIPs) 
after assessing their need- based requirements.  
 
5.2 Secretary, Health, during her presentation before the Committee, highlighted the 
main strategies for Maternal Health and stat ed that the main factors for high rate of 
MMR could be broadly classified as delay in decision-making, delay in reaching the 
hospital and delay in initiating appropriate care. Delay in decision-making takes place 
mainly due to lack of awareness and financ ial constraints. The Committee was informed 
that to tackle these basic constraints, a nu mber of significant interventions have been 
made so far. Awareness and counseling through ASHAs/ ANMs/ AWWs, availability of 
financial assistance through Janani Suraksha Yojana for institutional delivery, and skilled 
providers for domiciliary birth were cited as the main interventions. The other problem 
area coming in the way of checking MMR was delay in reaching the hospital due to lack 
of knowledge about danger signs and availability of transport facilities. Here again, 
ASHAs/ ANMs/ AWWs are meant to do the counseling on danger signs.  Transport 
facilities are being made available through JSY and referral services through 
Government ambulances/ PPP arrangements. Another hindrance noticed was lack of 
availability of operational facility for tracking pregnancy and its complications. The 
Committee was given to understand that these hindrances were being tackled by 
upgradation of infrastructure at the district level and multi-skilling of Doctors to 
overcome shortage of critical specialists. As  on date, 1024 doctors have been trained in 
anesthesia skills, 543 doctors trained in obstetric skills and 37,162 Staff nurses/ LHVs/ 
ANMs trained as skilled birth attendants.  
 
5.3 The Committee was also apprised about the main factors found responsible for 
high rate of IMR, which were severe infec tions (36% deaths), pre-term birth (25% 
deaths), birth asphyxia (23% deaths) and neo- natal tetanus (4% deaths). Steps taken so 
far for checking this high incidence of neo-na tal deaths were Integrated Management of 
New Born and Childhood Illness, Basic New Born Care and Navjat Shishu Suraksha 
Karyakaran etc. 
 
5.4 Allocation for RCH Flexible Pool for 2009-10 was Rs 3545.00 crore and the 
actual expenditure reported upto January , 2010 was Rs. 2641.84 crore. The Committee 
observes that there has been optimum utilization of allocated funds. Against a provision 

 104
of Rs. 1872.00 crore and Rs. 3126.04 crore in  2007-08 and 2008-09, utilization reported 
was Rs. 1999.25 crore and Rs. 3077.16 crore respectively. Committee’s attention has, 
however, been drawn by State-wise utilization status which depicts wide ranging 
disparities, that too in NE state specially as indicated below: 
 
 (Rs in crores) 
2007-08 2008-09 2009-10  
State Allocation Exp Allocation Exp Allocation Exp (upto 
31.12.09) 
Assam 166.84 105.24 240.33 194.74 338.62 110.37 
Manipur 14.24 7.85 21.50 15.45 30.29 6.15 
Meghalaya 13.87 4.95 21.13 6.87 29.29 4.98 
Nagaland 11.88 4.57 18.12 11.54 25.32 5.88 
Tripura 18.53 5.47 28.57 14.41 40.72 4.94 
 
5.5 Another disturbing trend noticed by  the Committee was that in respect of 
many States, Central funds released during th e year were higher than the proposed 
allocation. While trying to understand the reasons therefor, the Committee came to 
know that all the States/ UTs were having unspent balances at the beginning of the 
year. As per the details made available to the Committee, as on 01.04.2008, opening 
balance amounting to Rs. 1157.46 crore was available with Assam (Rs. 126.67 
crore), Maharashtra (Rs. 209.69 crore), Orissa (Rs. 60.99 crore), Tamil Nadu (Rs. 
97.73 crore), U.P. (Rs. 192.33 crore) and West Bengal (Rs. 101.59 crore) reporting 
the maximum. 
 
5.6 This clearly shows that States have generally failed to take the full benefit of 
various Central interventions inspite of their having the discretion to spend money 
as per their requirements. The Committee, ther efore, strongly feels that if despite 
the availability of funds the states are unable  to utilize them, and thus is a cause for 
a  thorough review of strategies. The low utilization of funds has seriously hampered 
Government’s initiatives for bringing down IMR, MMR and TFR. Seriously 
concerned with this state of affair s, the Committee recommends that the 
Department, in consultation with the St ates/ UTs, should devise alternative 
strategies so that the funds allocated are utilized optimally so that the poor and 
weaker sections of the society are not b ereft of the benefits of various on-going 
health care initiatives.  

 105
 
5.7 On being asked as to whether any assessment has been made so far about the 
main factors responsible for some of the High Focus States/NE States and Hill States of 
Himachal Pradesh and J&K failing to avail of maximum facilities under RCH Flexi pool 
the Department has highlighted the following main constraints:- 
- Shortage of Human resources in the relevant States 
- Difficult geographic conditions (NE states, J & K, Uttarakhand, HP) 
- Low absorption capacity 
- Absenteeism 
- Tenures of key officers like, Programme  Officers and Administrators are not 
assured, which affects programme ownership and continuity of interventions 
 
5.8 Following are the remedial measures suggested to States for inclusion in their 
State Programme Implementation Plans (PIPs). 
 
- Hiring health staff on contractual basis and local people 
- Providing hardship allowance to health staff  
- Capacity building of the State and district programme officers in decentralized 
planning and financial matters. 
 
 
5.9 The Committee desires to know wh ether the PIPs of the States under 
reference has started reflecting the deficien t areas as flagged off by the Department, 
and asking for funds to overcome them.  
 
ROUTINE IMMUNIZATION 
 
5.10 A provision of Rs. 2457.16 crore has been made for Routine Immunization during 
the Eleventh Plan. The Committee observes that funds allocated during the first three 
years have remained under-utilised with only less than 50 per cent being spent so far. 
 
5.11 The Committee was informed that ag ainst the budget allocation of Rs.466.30 
crore during the year 2008-09, a sum of Rs. 159.96 crore was utilized. Out of this Rs 
145.08 crore was utilized for procurement of vacc ines including Hepatitis B. Further, the 
saving was mainly under the sub-head ‘Vaccine  for Hepatitis B/Pentavalent Vaccine’ for 
which a fund of 205.30 crore was allocated ou t of which Rs. 21.16 crore could only be 
utilized as the scheme regarding introduction of Pentavalent Vaccine was deferred for 
technical re-examination of the issue. Out of the budget provision of Rs. 86.0 crore under 
Needle syringes and Rs. 47.0 crore under cold  chain equipment, a sum of Rs. 13.09 crore 

 106
and Rs. 1.79 crore was utilized respectively,  due to non-finalization /less procurement of 
these items.  
 
5.12 Allocation in 2010-11 has been increased to Rs 417 crore against an allocation of 
Rs 388.21 crore during 2009-10. The Committee was given to understand that the 
enhanced allocation of Rs 28.79 crores has been proposed for procurement of cold chain 
equipments and Hepatitis B/ Pentavalent vaccines. 
 
5.13 The Committee is constrained to observe that this is yet another case of inept 
handling of budgeted funds by the Department. The Committee finds to its dismay 
that despite the efficacy and relevance of  Pentavalent Vaccine being in question, a 
substantive amount of Rs. 205.30 crore was allocated for the same out of which      
Rs. 184.14 crore remained unutilised and had to be surrendered. The Committee 
would like the Department to exercise greater caution in future and not allow repeat 
of such fiscal imprudence.  
5.14 Asked to furnish the latest state-wise details of full immunization in the country, 
the Department has informed that as per latest DLHS III survey in 2007-08 full 
immunization coverage of children (12-23 mo nths) in the country increased to 54.1% 
compared to 45.9% as per the DLHS-II (2002 -04).  However, in some of the States, 
percentage of coverage still remains much below the national average:

5.15 The Committee notes that population left out because of poor access to health 
care facilities has been cited the main factor responsible for such a situation. The 

 107
Committee has been given to understand that a number of initiatives have been taken by 
the Department which include:

- Social Mobilization to ensure universal access. 
- Support for Cold chain maintenance 
- Quarterly Review meetings for programme monitoring. 
- Mobility Support to assist active supervision. 
 
 
5.16 From a perusal of the information made available, the Committee would like 
to state here that significant progress un der the Immunisation Programme has not 
been noticed in many of the States. This fa ct holds true both for the high focus and 
EAG States and also the non-high focus States .  In some instances like that of Tamil 
Nadu and Dadra & Nagar Haveli, the immu nization coverage has infact dropped 
below the DLHS II rate. The Committee observes that a low coverage rate in some 
of the very densely populated States like Bihar and Uttar Pradesh means that a very 
large number of children remain exposed to the vagaries of diseases resulting in 
high incidence of IMR in the country. The Committee observes that unless proper 
execution of the plans are made by the programme authorities, visible impact on the 
immunization coverage would remain a distant dream. It, therefore, urges the 
Department to take up the issue with th e respective State Mission Directors during 
the next Common Review Mission, who need to be impressed upon to achieve the 
required quarterly/ annual targets. 
 
5.17 Cold chain maintenance is the mo st crucial component for effective 
implementation of the Immunisation  Programme. The Committee, however, 
observes that so far this has proved to be the weakest link under the programme. 
Committee’s apprehensions have been confirmed by the assessment of cold chain 
maintenance arrangement made under the Performance Audit of CAG. As per the 
CAG Audit, none of the cold chain equipment was available in any of the 124 test 
checked PHCs in Haryana, Meghalaya, Madhya Pradesh, Puducherry and West 
Bengal. Besides, in Bihar, Lakshadweep and Uttar Pradesh, 31 to 68 per cent of cold 
chain equipment was found non-functional. 
 

 108
5.18 The Committee is more disturbed by  the fact that Government of India 
budgetary support for maintenance of cold chain equipments, supply of spare parts, 
provision of alternate power supply has prove d to be of no avail. The States have 
simply confirmed their inability to abso rb the funds for envisaged purposes. The 
Committee is of the view that the absence of cold chain management has indirectly 
resulted in adverse impact on the UIP, a high priority area under the Mission. 
 
PULSE POLIO PROGRAMME 
 
5.19 Under Pulse Polio Programme, Rs 1150.94 crore were allocated in BE 2009-10 
which was revised upwards to Rs 1191.31 crore at the RE stage, with full utilization by 
the end of the year. 
 
5.20 The Committee was informed that the increased allocation during 2009-10 at RE 
stage was based on the number of rounds i.e. two National Immunization Day (NIDs),        
7 Sub-National Immunization Days (SNIDs) and 3 Mop-Up. As compared to 2009-10, a 
reduced provision of Rs. 1017.08 crore has been  made for 2010-11. It has been clarified 
that during 2010-11, based on the epidemiological picture of polio infection being 
confined to western Uttar Pradesh and Kosi region of Bihar, the area of operation has 
reduced and hence the reduced allocation would not affect the on-going activities.  
 
5.21 The Committee has been informed that currently type 3 & type 1 virus was in 
circulation, limited mainly to 107 high risk blocks of Uttar Pradesh and Bihar. High 
density populations, poor hygiene practices, nearly non-existent sanitation infrastructure, 
compromised drinking water supply, inadequate health and routine immunization 
services, high malnutrition rates, and povert y have been cited as the main factors 
responsible for such a scenario. A multi- pronged strategy is being planned which 
includes cross-cutting activities to minimize the factors facilitating poliovirus 
transmission in these 107 blocks while at the same time ensuring the highest possible 
quality of polio immunization activities.  
 
5.22 As a special intervention, the new-born tracking is being done in U.P. and Bihar 
for coverage during pulse polio round upto the age of one year, (now been extended to all 
the States). It has also stated that although in 2009, a total of 602 polio cases were 
reported compared to 305 cases in 2008 from Uttar Pradesh, it may be noted that only 34 

 109
cases were due to more virulent Wild Polio Virus (WPV)-1 type as against 62 cases of 
WPV-1 in 2008.   
 
5.23 The Committee is happy to note that the Department has been diligently 
pressing forward with various measures to contain the spread of WPV-1 and WPV-
3 types, especially entrenched in Biha r and Uttar Pradesh. The Committee hopes 
that the introduction of the bivalent vacci ne (P1& P3) during 2010 would go a long 
way in eradicating polio from the country.  Though the Department has cited that 
the absolute numbers of polio cases have been reducing over the years, given the 
massive scale on which the programme is being implemented with NIDs, SNIDs and 
mop-up rounds reported polio cases have shown no significant improvement during 
the past three years. The Committee woul d like to reckon here that as per the 
findings of DLHS III (2007-08) survey, th e all India figures of polio immunization 
stand at 66.2% only, with Bihar (53.1%), UP (44.4%), Madhya Pradesh (55.1%), 
Mehghalaya (46.5%), Jharkhand (64.4%), Ra jasthan (63.9%)  and most of the NE 
States performing less than the all India average. Given the above levels of PPI 
coverage in various states, the Committee would like the Department to explain us 
as to how the goal of zero transmission can be achieved at the earliest.  
 
 
JANANI SURAKSHA YOJANA 
5.24 Janani Suraksha Yojana (JSY) launched on 12 th April, 2005 a safe motherhood 
intervention under NRHM being implemented with the objective of promoting 
institutional delivery among the poor pregnant women. Being implemented in all 
States/UTs JSY as a 100 per cent Centrally Sponsored Scheme and it integrates JSY 
benefits with delivery and post-delivery care.  
 
5.25 The Yojana has identified ASHA as the main link between the Government and 
the poor pregnant women, whose main role is to facilitate pregnant women to avail 
maternal care services and a rrange referral transport. In  Low Performing States, all 
women, including SCs and STs opting for getting deliveries conducted in Government 
Health Care Centres/Institutions or accredited private institutions are eligible to receive 
cash assistance. In High Performing States, BPL women and SC and ST women are 
eligible to receive cash assistance under the Yojana.  
The Scale of cash assistance for Institutional Delivery is as under:-

5.26 The Committee notes that JSY, as a financial package, has done well to improve 
institutional deliveries. Institutional deliveries have increased from 7.30 lakh in 2005-06 
to 84.26 lakh in the year 2008-09.  However, CAG in its Performance Audit of NRHM 
(2009-10) has pointed out a number of shortcomings in the implementation of JSY, out of 
which some are as follows:- 
(i) 
  In 13 out of 20 States, less than 50 per cent of total registered pregnant women

(ii)  Proper records and vouchers in support of payment of incentive were not always

delay have been pointed out by CAG.  
(iii) Quarterly physical and financial report s to be furnished by States/UTs were not 
being regularly sent. 
(iv) There were variations in the quarte rly/annual reports and Financial Management 
Reports sent by field nodal officers of JSY and State Health Societies.  The CAG 
also mentioned that the Ministry's data on institutional deliveries and number of 
beneficiaries was inconsistent with the da ta verified during audit. According to 
Quarterly Report upto December, 2009 sent to Audit by the Ministry, number of 
beneficiaries of domiciliary deliveries was 15.95 lakh which came down to 11.30 
lakh at the end of the year.  
 
5.27 The Committee views with serious concern the high level of pregnant women 
registered for institutional delivery not op ting for the same. This clearly indicates 
that various interventions made under JSY so as to make aware the targeted 
population about the positive impact of institutional delivery both on the mother 

 111
and child have not proved to be very eff ective. Situation becomes more worrisome 
by the fact that high focus States where need was the most have been showing such 
a trend. The Committee fails to understand the reasons behind ground level 
functionaries not monitoring the trimest er check-up of the registered pregnant 
women. The Committee would like to point out that the achievement of 47 per cent 
of institutional deliveries (DLHS-III 2007- 08) from 37 percent (DLHS-I  1998-99) 
although indicates a slow but steady increase, however, one must not forget that 53 
per cent women still continue to remain de prived of the benefits of institutional 
deliveries. The Committee is of the view that the role of ASHA/ ANM under the JSY 
seems to be limited to payment of cash incentives only. Consistent tracking of 
expectant mother for antenatal and post-partum check-up and deliveries needed 
more focused attention. For this, regular monitoring and surprise inspections need 
to be included as an essential component of JSY. 
5.28 The Committee has also been informed that an Evaluation Study of JSY is 
being conducted by an Expert Group led by the Executive Director, National Health 
Systems Resource Centre (NHSRC) with inter-alia the following objectives: 
 
(a) Procedure for random verification of beneficiaries; 
(b) Fund Flow mechanism for administering JSY scheme and mechanism 
adopted for ensuring timely payment to the beneficiaries; and 
(c) Impact of JSY services on maternal mortality ratio and infant mortality rate. 
 
The Committee would like to be informed about the latest status in this regard.  
 
5.29 The Committee was informed that the Government is actively pursuing the goal 
of reduction in Maternal Mortality by focu sing on the 4 major strategies of essential 
obstetric and new born care for all, skilled at tendance at every birth, emergency obstetric 
care and referral services. However, these critical components which figure among the 
four major strategies adopted for reducing MMR suffer from grave inadequacies.  
Operationalisation of 50 per cent PHCs for 24x7 Services is the most important initiative 
under Essential Obstetric Care. However, status of 24x7 PHCs in States having very high 
Maternal Mortality is very discouraging as indicated below:

5.31 Asked to indicate the reasons for such a dismal scenario, the Department has 
stated that MMR of poor performing States has seen considerable decline during the 
period 2004-06 as compared to the MMR during the period 2001-03. In the area of 
Essential Obstetric Care, Referral Services and Skilled Attendants at Birth, there has been 
considerable improvement in the poor perform ing States also.  However, owing to acute 
shortage of manpower, these States are not in a position to relieve the trainees at time for 
getting multi-skilled or even for upgradation of their existing skills.  
 
5.32 The Committee observes that the goal of reducing MMR to 100 per lakh like 
births by 2012 is very difficult to achieve at the present rate of decline. The goal can 
be achieved only if there is rapid ex pansion of Skilled Birth Attendants and 

 113
Emergency Obstetric Care (EmOC) . It is in this context th at deficiencies in EmOC 
and Skilled Birth Attendance are very worrisome.  
 
5.33 The Committee is of the view that in the absence of required ground-level 
health functionaries who have to be qual ified, trained and semi-skilled personnel, 
the outreach of JSY and ot her specific interventions wi ll remain confined to the 
barest minimum. Number of ambitious schemes with high objectives and 
availability of massive funding will not succeed in reaching the targeted 
beneficiaries. Close monitori ng, not on paper but at the ground level, in the real 
sense alone can strengthen the effective implementation of all the initiatives taken so 
far. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
VI. NATIONAL VECTOR BORNE DISEASE CONTROL PROGRAMME                                  
(NVBDCP) 
 
 
6.1 The National Vector Borne Disease Control Programme is a comprehensive 
programme for prevention and control of vector  borne diseases namely Malaria, Filaria, 
Kala-azar, Japanese Encephalitis (JE), Deng ue and Chikununya which is covered under 
the overall umbrella of NRHM. Central Gove rnment provides technical and financial 
assistance and also logistics support and the State Governments have to meet the other 

 114
requirements and operational costs. NE States are provided 100 per cent central 
assistance under the programme.  
6.2 The general strategy for prevention an d control of vector borne diseases under 
NVBDCP is through Disease Management,  Integrated Vector Management  and 
Supportive Interventions.  The Committee was informed that for the year 2010-2011, 
the Directorate of NVBDCP has projected their demands amounting to Rs.698 crore. 
However, allocation of Rs.418 crore has been  made considering overall allocation of 
Ministry of Health & Family Welfare. Out of the allocation of Rs. 418.00 crore for 2010-
11, the EAP component is Rs 165.26 crore (GFATM 84.87 crore and World Bank 80.39 
crore).  
6.3 The Committee notes that against an allocation of Rs. 442.00 crore in 2009-
10, expenditure reported as on 28.02. 2010 was only Rs. 278.61 crore. The 
Committee observes that this trend of under-utilisation of allocated fund is also 
there in the first two years of the Eleventh  Plan. Against the provision of Rs. 399.50 
crore and Rs. 472.25 crore in 2007-08 a nd 2008-09, expenditure was only Rs. 383.66 
crore and Rs.  299.38 crore respectively. Th e Committee is constrained to observe 
that against the total Plan outlay of Rs. 3190.00 crore for the Eleventh Plan, less 
than half of the funds were released and that too remained under-utilised. 
Committee’s repeated attempts for optimum utilization of allocated funds under 
NVBDCP has failed to make any impact. 
6.4 The Committee has always been responsive to the genuine demands of the 
Department. The Committee would, however, also like the Department to focus on 
better utilization of the scarce resource s. The Committee, once again, recommends 
that the Department need to focus on analyzing the reasons for reduced utilization 
of the allocated funds and take corrective action accordingly.  
 
The Committee would also like to know whether any commitment charges 
are to be paid by the Department in case of shortfall in utilization of EAP 
component and if so, the charges paid during the last three years. 
 
MALARIA 
 
6.5 The National Health Policy (2002) has set the goal of reduction in mortality on 
account of malaria by 50 percent by 2010. The Committee was informed that reported 
Malaria cases declined from 1.78 million in 2006 to 1.53 million in 2008 and deaths from 

 115
Malaria declined from 1708 in 2006 to 1055 in 2008.  Still it  continued to be a major 
public health challenge in the North East States, Orissa, Jharkhand, West Bengal, and 
Chhatisgarh.  In 2008, these states accounted for 65 percent of the total reported Malaria 
cases,   85 percent of total Pf cases and 68 percent of total  reported malaria deaths.    
  
6.6 It is also informed that though  the number of reported Pf cases in the country has  
declined but  out of the total mala ria cases reported,  proportion of Plasmodium 
falciparum cases has increased from 44.32% in 2005  to 52.09% in 2009 (Prov.) As per 
the latest  information furnished by the Department, in 2009, Meghalaya (with 192 
deaths), Orissa (192), Mizoram (119), Tripura (62), West Bengal (74), Maharashtra 
(159), Nagaland (35), and Assam (63) were leading the table for malaria-related deaths. 
6.7  The Committee notes that the number of  cases reported is already on the 
rise in many of the States, especially the North-Eastern States, where the problem is 
acute. Under the GFATM project all the 77 district in 7 NE States, 7 districts of 
Jharkhand, 6 districts of West Bengal and 16  districts of Orissa are covered.  The 
Committee is not fully convinced by the Departments, contention that due to remote 
and inaccessible areas with poor access to malaria diagnosis and treatment facilities, 
NE states continue to remain a major cha llenge.  The Committee would like to state 
here that the programme has been these for considerable period and with 100 
percent central funding and also Internatio nal assistance, these challenges, should 
have been significantly lessened. The Comm ittee also finds that major interventions 
like introduction of Rapid diagnostic kits , for the field workers, introduction of 
specialized therapy for Pf cases and long lasting insecticidal nets have all been made 
for North East States.  However, inspite of such huge funding under the programme 
significant progress is not visible. The Committee can only conclude that 
supervision and monitoring, which is one of the main strategies adopted for effective 
control of malaria, is perhaps not being implemented to the desired levels, especially 
in NE region and Orissa.  
6.8 The Committee is of the view that shortage of manpower is the main stumbling 
block in the successful implementation of malaria control measures. The Committee has 
been given to understand that for strengthening supervision and monitoring, additional 
manpower like consultants at state level, Vect or Borne Diseases Consultants at district 
level and Malaria Technical Supervisor at sub district level have been provided in high 
endemic areas under the World Bank and GFATM supported projects.   

 116
 
6.9 The Committee would however, like to draw the attention of the Department 
to the status of some key functionaries under Malaria control programme. The 
Committee is astonished to find that inspit e of special attention being given to the 
NE States important functionaries are simply not there as indicated below: 
- Vector borne Disease Control (DVBOC) Consultant is not there in any of 
the                 NE States and Sikkim.  
- Only 522 PHC-MOs are posted in Arunachal Pradesh. Remaining States 
do not have this functionary. 
- Assam does not have a Lab-Technician/ Lab-Technician(Contractual)  
- Both Assam and Nagaland do not have a Health Supervisor (M) 
- No Health Supervisor (F) is post ed in Arunachal Pradesh, Assam, 
Nagaland, Sikkim and Tripura. 
- Multi-Purpose Worker (M) is not there in Arunachal Pradesh and Assam. 
- Only Nagaland has 28 Malaria Inspectors 
- Surveillance Staff (307) is there only in Meghalaya. 
- Only Meghalaya has 54 Spray Squads. 
- In Sikkim and Tripura Malaria Tec hnical Supervisors (Contractual) are 
not  there  
- n Arunachal Pradesh and Assam there are no regular MPW (M) at all  
 
6.10 The fact that these functionaries play a vital role in disease detection and 
surveillance cannot be set aside. By not appointing these functionaries, the States 
leave a large gap in the field-level disease surveillance and treatment. The 
Committee can only conclude from the above that it would be impractical to expect 
noticeable improvements in malaria control with key functionaries missing, that too, 
in the malaria endemic States.   Hence, the Committee advises the Department that, 
apart from allocating sufficient funds to these States/ UTs, it should also pursue 
with State Governments to fill up the vacancies without delay so that  the targets set 
to control malaria could be met within a realistic time-frame. The Committee would 
like the Department to furnish details of various measures adopted by the States 
Governments in the past year to fill up these vacancies. 
 
 
KALA-AZAR 
 
6.11 The National Health Policy (2002)  has se t the goal for elimination of    Kala-azar 
from the country by 2010. Kala-azar is endemic in four States - Bihar, West Bengal, 
Jharkhand and U.P.  More than 80 percent of  cases and over 90 percent of deaths are 
reported from Bihar.  340 out of 523 Block PHCs, which are Kala-azar endemic reported 
less than one case of Kala-azar per 10,000 populations in 2009.  

 117
 
6.12 During 2010, 2534 Kala-azar cases in Bihar, 59 in West Bengal and 1 case in U.P 
have been reported.  Strategies for Kala-a zar elimination include Vector Control (with 
DDT spray), upscaling use of new diagnostic tools i.e. rK39 , use of new oral drug 
Miltefosine as the first line of treatment in 10 pi lot districts of Bihar, West Bengal and 
Jharkhand.  For Bihar, the most endemic stat e pucca houses for the poorest of the poor 
were being constructed to prevent the breeding of the Vector.  
 
6.13 The Committee observes that though the National Health Policy (2002) had 
set the goal of elimination of Kala-azar by 2010, the above target is yet to be 
realized. This is evident from the fact th at 2534 reported cases of Kala-azar from 
the States of Bihar, West Bengal and Utta r Pradesh have been reported during the 
first two months of the year 2010. It is in  this context that two rounds of door-to-
door search which were to be undertaken  for elimination of Kala-azar, assume 
added significance. However, the Committee fails to understand as to why Kala-
azar fortnights could not be observed in  the most endemic States of Bihar and 
Jharkhand during the past year.  Inspit e of the Committee’s repeated comments on 
carrying on intensive door-to-door surveill ance and monitoring by the Department 
the situation so far has been far from encouraging.  The Committee would like to be 
enlightened about the reasons behind not undertaking the door-to-door search in 
Bihar, Uttar Pradesh. The Committee rec ommends that effective surveillance, 
timely treatment and compliance of treatmen t regimens in all PHCs in the affected 
districts need to be continued without any let-up.  
 
6.14 The Committee would like the Department to apprise it in clear terms the 
number of PHCs in the endemic districts that have been provided with anti-Kala 
azar drugs, the number of household/ population covered district-wise under the 
door-to-door surveillance activities, and the physical outcomes of such initiatives. 
The Committee would also like to be  apprised about the number of NGOs/ 
FBOs/PRIs involved in Kala-azar eliminatio n activities in the above States, the 
funds provided to them during the last th ree years, the cases treated by them and 
the initiatives taken by them to ensure treatment compliance of the patients.

is endemic in 14 States out of which, Assam, Bihar, Haryana and Uttar Pradesh have 
reported repeated outbreaks. Over the past 5 years, maximum number of cases had been 
reported in the year 2005. The number of cases detected and d eaths during 2007,  2008 
and 2009 are given below:
