6.16 There is no specific anti-viral drug for JE and cases are managed 
symptomatically. A vaccination programme for children between 1 to 15 years of age 
under the Universal Immunization Programme, using single dose vaccine has been 
launched in 2006 and ever since, 85 JE endemi c districts in the States of Assam, Andhra 
Pradesh, Bihar, Haryana, Goa, Karnataka, Kerala, Maharashtra, Tamil Nadu, Uttar 
Pradesh and West Bengal have been covered under the programme.  
6.17 On being asked by the Committee regarding the details of Immunization 
Coverage in UP, the most endemic state Department has stated that as per a recent report 
of the UNICEF on the reported JE vaccinati on coverage in Gorakhpur and Bahraich 
districts of Uttar Pradesh, the coverage was found to be only 51 and 52 percent 
respectively in these two districts. Realizing that many eligible children were left out 
during 2006 campaign conducted in Uttar Pradesh, a special JE vaccination campaign for 
left out children in all seven most sensitive districts of U.P covered during 2006 is under 
the consideration of Government of India.  The Committee notes that the overall figures 
of immunization for 2009 stand at 46.62 per cent only. A perusal of the state-wise 
coverage reveals that the vaccination coverage in West Bengal has been far from 
satisfactory with 56 per cent  in 2006, 74 per cent in 2007, 57 per cent in 2008 and less 

 119
than 30 per cent (provisional) during 2009. What is astonishing is that out of 41,34,072 
children (between 1-15 years) targeted in four districts from Andhra Pradesh  no children 
could be vaccinated , similarly, in Bihar out of the 13,62,508 children targeted in Gaya 
District and in Tamil Nadu 15,52,386 children targeted in two districts, none could be 
vaccinated. The  
 
6.18 The Committee is dismayed to note that  more than four years have elapsed 
since the vaccination programme was launched in 2006, yet the all India coverage of 
JE vaccination campaign is only 46.62% uptill 2009 as against the targeted 85% 
coverage. What is more worrisome is that JE vaccination coverage in four districts 
of Andhra Pradesh, one district of Assam and two districts of Tamil Nadu is nil, 
which paints a very grim picture, especially  in the context of there being no specific 
anti-viral drug for JE. The Committee feels that if such gaps are not addressed 
immediately, efforts to contain JE outbreaks will stand jeopardized. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that th e Department should, in coordination 
with the State Government concerned, chal k out an action plan to achieve the 
targeted coverage of JE vaccination and also apprise the Committee of the progress 
made in this regard. Similar efforts for vacc ine coverage of the affected districts of 
Andhra Pradesh, Assam and Tamil Nadu are also required.   
6.19 The Committee would also like to be apprised of the progress made towards 
establishing sentinel sites for regular surveillance and Apex Referral Laboratories 
for advanced diagnosis, circulation of stan dard guidelines, capacity building and 
intensive IEC campaign and vaccination with single dose under Universal 
Immunization Programme. Keeping in view th e fact that Uttar Pradesh is the most 
endemic State which contributes 80% of JE cases, the Committee recommends that 
the Government must quickly take a decision on conducting a special JE 
vaccination campaign for left out children in the seven most sensitive districts of 
Uttar Pradesh along with the affected di stricts of Andhra Pradesh, Assam and 
Tamil Nadu. The quarterly progress made in this regard may be communicated to 
the Committee. 
6.20 The Committee was also informed that apart from establishing field unit of NIV, 
Pune at BRD Medical College, Gorakhpur for de tecting and isolating non-JE viruses, the 
management of AES/JE cases has been accorde top priority. Prevention and control 
guidelines have already been circulated to all the affected districts in the State for 

 120
initiating control measures against entero-vir uses. The diagnostic facilities have been 
strengthened at 50 sentinel and 13 Apex Refe rral Laboratories including 15 sentinel sites 
established in Uttar Pradesh. These have been supplied with diagnostic kits free of cost 
from National Institute of Virology (NIV), Pune. One Vector Borne Disease Surveillance 
Unit (VBDSU) and one JE sub-office was established at BRD Medical College, 
Gorakhpur, Uttar Pradesh. Lastly, for establis hing a Physical, Medicine & Rehabilitation 
(PMR) department at BRD Medical College for treating physical disabilities due to 
AES/JE, MOU has been submitted to the State of Uttar Pradesh. 
6.21 The Committee is happy to note that finally some action to check 
aggravation of non-JE viruses in Eastern UP  have been initiated by the Central 
Government. It can only hope that the situation would improve this year during the 
outbreak season. The Committee hopes th at the manpower required to make the 
sentinel sites, referral labs operational and the Diagnostic kits provided free of cost 
by National Institute of Virology (NIV), Pune, are in position.
 
 
VII NATIONAL TB CONTROL PROGRAMME  
 
7.1 Tuberculosis is a major public health problem in India. The country accounts for 
nearly one-fifth of the global incidence. In 2007, out of the global annual incidence of 
9.23 million TB cases, 1.96 million were estimated to have occurred in India, of whom 
0.8 million were infectious cases. About tw o persons die from TB in India every three 
minutes; 900 people every day and almost 3.28 lakh every year. The Committee was 
informed that the Revised National TB C ontrol Programme (RNTCP) has been extended 
to the entire country in 2006. Till date, 11 million T.B. patients have been initiated on 
treatment with 2 million additional lives saved. 
  
 7.2 To tackle HIV/TB co-infection, TB + HIV collaborative activities with 
NACP, initiated in 2001, have been scaled up across the country. The Committee 
notes that intensified TB/HIV package of services have been started in nine HIV 
high prevalence States (Andhra Pradesh, Goa, Karnataka, Maharashtra, Manipur, 
Mizoram, Nagaland, Pondicherry and Tamil Nadu) in 2008 and has been extended 
to nine additional States (Delhi, Gujarat, Punjab, Rajasthan, Kerala, Assam, West 
Bengal, Orissa and Chandigarh) in 2009-10 with a vision to cover the entire nation 
by 2012. Monitoring of co-infected patients was started from 4
th quarter of 2008, 

 121
and out of a total of 31047 such cases, 5171 cases were treated with a 75 per cent 
success rate. The Committee is happy to note that the Department has been pursuing its 
concerns diligently to identify and provide treatment to HIV/T.B co-infected patients. 
  
7.3 To control this major health problem of the country, Revised National TB Control 
Programme (RNTCP) based on internationa lly recommended strategy of Directly 
Observed Treatment Short Course (DOTS) . The Programme has the objective of 
detecting at least 70% of cases and curing at  least 85% of sputum positive patients. In 
order to address the problem of Multi Drug Resistance TB (MDR-TB), establishment of a 
network of 43 accredited Culture and Drug  Susceptibility Testing Laboratories for 
diagnosis and follow-up of MDR TB patients across the country was being undertaken in 
a phased manner. It is planned to roll out se rvices for Drug Resistant Tuberculosis in all 
State/ UTs by March 2011. 
7.4 The Committee observes that the emergence of multi-drug resistant TB has 
increased the severity and magnitude of the problem in terms of cost, longer and 
difficult treatment regimen. In this contex t, the Committee welc omes the initiative 
of establishing a network of 43 Laboratories for diagnosis and follow-up of MDRTB 
patients. The Committee hopes that such Laboratories will be set up across the 
country in such a way that people from all parts of the country have equity in access 
to such facilities. The Committee would like to have an idea of the location of such 
Laboratories and also of the structure of charges, if any, proposed to levied for 
availing of the facilities therefrom. 
7.5 The Committee observes that utilizati on status of allocated funds under the 
programme has been satisfactory. For 2009-10, against the total allocation of Rs. 296.25 
crore an expenditure of Rs. 294.83 crore has been made till 12
th March, 2010. The 
Committee is however, surprised to observe that unspent balances are available with all 
the States/ UTs in the beginning of the year. The opening balance of Rs. 32.20 crore as 
on 01.04.2009 has reached the figure of Rs. 79.19 crore by 31 st December, 2009. 
Following States show the maximum amount of unspent balances.

The Committee would, therefore, like th e Department to at least outline the 
roadmap for reducing, if not entirely wiping out, the extent of unspent balances. 
7.7 The Committee takes note of the fact that out of a total outlay of Rs. 1156 
crore for the Programme, Rs. 756.00 crore is the credit from World Bank. The

financial implications by way of debt or commitment charges and if so, the extent of 
financial liability involved so far.

involved under RNTCP. The Committee would like to be apprised of the extent of 
financial assistance extended to the NGOs, the amount utilized, the number of 
pending UCs from them and the amount involved therein. 
 
 7.9 To the Committee’s query regarding the latest status on the new drug for treating 
patient with MDR-TB being developed by M/s Johnson and Johnson, the Department has 
informed that the new molecule (TMC 207) is currently undergoing Phase II clinical 
trials to evaluate its efficacy, safety and tolerability in patients suffering from MDR-TB. 
Tuberculosis Research Centre, Chennai and Al l India Institute of Medical Sciences are 
participating in this trial which is being unde rtaken in 17 countries across the world. The 
details on the cost, affordability, efficacy etc. will be available only after successful 
completion of the clinical trials and once the drug is approved for marketing by the 
regulatory authorities. The Committee would like the Department to furnish the latest 
status on the Phase II clinical trials and its findings. 
 

 123
7.10 Committee’s attention has also been drawn by Drug Resistance Surveys (DRS) 
undertaken in Gujarat and Maharashtra in 2005-06, which have estimated the prevalence 
of Multi-drug Resistant TB (MDR-TB) to be around 3% amongst new TB cases and 14-
17% amongst                re-treatment TB cases. It has also been informed that in Andhra 
Pradesh the study has been completed and the results of which were expected to be 
available by Second quarter of 2010.  In    U.P. and Orissa similar surveys were going on. 
 
7.11 The Committee would like the Department to share with it the state-wise 
details of the Survey Reports while submitti ng its action taken note. The Committee  
has been given to understand that a ba seline social assessment study at the 
beginning of the RNTCP II project had b een undertaken to assess some of the 
predisposing factors contributing to the spread of the disease, and that another such 
survey is being planned to be made in 2010-11 which will also include  evaluation of 
the predisposing factors for spread of TB.  The findings of the baseline survey may 
be shared with the Committee.  
 
 
VIII. NATIONAL LEPROSY ERADICATION PROGRAMME 
 
8.1 The goal of leprosy elimination at the Na tional level is less than 1 case per 10000 
population. National level elimination of Leprosy has been achieved since 31
st December, 
2005 when the prevelance rate came down to 0.95 or less than 1/10000 population. 32 
States/ UTs have achieved the status of leprosy elimination. Only 3 States/ UT i.e. Bihar, 
Chhattisgarh and Dadra & Nagar Haveli are ye t to achieve elimination level, which have 
the prevalence rate of 1.07, 2.30 and 2.21 respectively. 
                                             
8.2 The Committee was informed that in Bihar & Chhattisgarh reduction in 
prevalence of leprosy started late, due to late introduction of Multi Drug Therapy (MDT) 
for treatment of leprosy.  D & N Haveli being an industrial area has large number of 
migratory cases from nearby States/UTs, and du e to less population, a small increase in 
the number of recorded cases resulted in enormous increase in the prevalence rate.   
 
8.3 The Committee takes note of a number of preventive measures like involvement 
of ASHAs, focused attention in endemic blocks, urban areas, training of all General 
Health Services functionaries for leprosy diagnosis and management and Intensified 
Information, Education & Communication (IEC). 

 124
 
8.4 While appreciating the steady progress in the elimination of leprosy from the 
endemic states, the Committee would like the Department to closely monitor the 
efficacy of the measures taken and intro duce suitable interven tions for elimination 
disease leprosy in D&N Haveli.  
 
8.5 On a specific query the Committee was informed that against a Budgetary 
allocation of    Rs. 44.50 crores for 2009-10, till 31
st December, 2009, an expenditure Rs 
27.35 crore has been made. However, as per the background material submitted by the 
Department, upto January, 2010 expenditure reported is Rs. 26.66 crore. Committee’s 
attention has also been drawn by unspent ba lances available under the programme with 
different States. 
 
8.6 The Committee notes that as on December, 2009 unspent balances amounting to 
Rs. 22.12 crore were available with States like Bihar (Rs. 1.34 crore), Madhya Pradesh 
(Rs. 1.49 crore), Maharashtra (Rs. 1.67 crore) , Orissa (Rs. 1.77 crore), Uttarakhand (Rs. 
2.92 crore) and West Bengal (Rs. 1.39 crore) having the maximum balance. 
   
8.7 Committee’s attention is especially drawn to the utilisation status shown 
against Bihar. The Committee notes that ne arly Rs. 2.67 crore were lying unspent 
with the state uptill March, 2008. However,  inspite of non release of funds during 
2008-09 and 2009-10 to the State, the unspent balances had to be carried forward 
from March, 2008 and could not be exhausted completely during the next two years 
with Rs. 1.34 crore still remaining by     31 st December, 2009. Similarly, in case of 
Orissa, Uttarachal, Jharkhand, Dadra & Nagar Haveli, West Bengal and 
Maharashtra large sums of money have been carried forward unspent from the 
previous year. The Committee feels that given the trend over the past couple of 
years, this year too, substantial amount of funds would lie under utilised/ unutilised.  
 
8.8 The Committee is of the considered view that judicious utilization of funds is 
indispensable for sound financial administrati on without which it would be difficult 
for the Department to obtain the targeted results under NLEP. The Committee is 
compelled to conclude that much needs to be done especially in the endemic States 
of Bihar, Jharkand and the UT of Dadra & Nagar Haveli. The Committee, 
therefore, urges the Department to take up the matter of fund utilisation on an 

 125
emphatic note with the State Programme Officers during its quarterly review 
meetings. The Committee would like the De partment to find out the underlying 
causes of underutilization of funds and take corrective action accordingly. 
 
8.9 The Committee has been monitoring the progress made in providing incentive of 
Rs.5000/- for Reconstructive Surgery to leprosy disabled persons from BPL families and 
to 20 institutions for conducting RCS. List of 52 recognized Reconstructive Surgery 
Centres (20 Govt. & 32 NGO) was also communicated to the States/ UTs. The States 
were further requested to identify more ce ntres for conducting RCS.  As per the latest 
information given by the Department, 26 more centres have been recognized thus making 
total of 78 centres for carrying out Recons tructive Surgery (40 Govt. & 38 NGOs) in 
leprosy. As reported by 14 States/UTs, 965 le prosy affected persons belonging to BPL 
family have been paid incentive for undergoing RCS.  
 
8.10 The Committee is happy to note that at last such an important scheme under 
NLEP has finally seen the light of day. Ho wever, with more than three years of the 
Eleventh Plan Period having passed, the Co mmittee can only hope that the benefits 
of the scheme would accrue to more number of beneficiaries during the coming 
years. The Committee would like to add a word of caution to the Department 
regarding having an effective monitoring mechanism so as to determine the veracity 
of the beneficiaries from initial stage itself. The Committee would also like to have 
an idea about the number of beneficiaries (State-wise). 
 
 
IX NATIONAL TRACHOMA AND BLINDNESS CONTROL PROGRAMME 
 
9.1 The National Programme for Control of  Blindness (NPCB) was launched in the 
year 1976 as a 100% Centrally-Sponsored scheme with the goal to reduce the prevalence 
of blindness from 1.4% to 0.3% by 2020. Rapid Survey on Avoidable Blindness 
conducted during NPCB during 2006-07 showed reduction in the prevalence of blindness 
from 1.1% (2001-02) to 1% (2006-07).  
9.2 The Committee observes that there has been optimum utilization of allocated 
funds under the programme during the Eleventh Plan.  During 2009-10 also full 
utilization of fund allocation of Rs. 250.00 crore was reported.  The Committee is 
however surprised to note that achievement level of physical targets has not matched the 
pace of fund utilization.  Following details are self-revealing.

Theatres against the target of 20, clearly indicates that things are not moving inspite of 
funds being duly allocated.

properly evaluate the reasons for these undera chievements so that the weak links in 
the chain of implementation of the initiatives can be id entified. This, the Committee 
believes, would surely result in addressing implementation of various components of 
the programme effectively. The Committee feels that if the situation continues to 
remain as tardy as now then the goals set by the Department under NPCB by the 
end of Eleventh plan would remain a distant dream.  
 
9.6 In reply to a specific query regarding the monitoring mechanism under NPCB for 
monitoring of Surgical Eye Camps, the Department has informed that such eye camps are 
not permitted under NPCB. However, Screening Eye Camps are conducted by 
NGOs/private Service Providers in the States  with the approval of the concerned State 
Health authorities under their monitoring and strict supervision. The performance of 
these camps is reviewed at district, state and central level meetings. However, the above 
information is contrary to the findings reported in the latest CAG Performance Audit of 
NRHM (2009-10), as per which in 14 Stat es/UTs, 19.52 lakh cataract surgeries were 
performed in camps during the period 2005-08 which was 47 per cent of total cataract 
surgeries in these States. Position in the following States in quite alarming:

9.7 The Committee is appalled to note that cataract operations in such huge 
proportions were allowed to be performed in eye camps between 2005-08, although, 
Government of India has banned eye operat i o n s  i n  c a m p s  o w i n g  t o  h i g h  r a t e  o f  
mishaps in such camps.  The Committee feel s that the issue cannot simply be done 
away with, especially in the light of the findings of the CAG. The Committee fails to 
understand as to how, insp ite of the claims made by the Department regarding 
regular monitoring of compliance to the guidelines, cataract surgeries continued to 
be performed in camps in the aforesaid states. The Committee apprehends that such 
surgeries in Camps must be continuing even at present thereby putting a large 
number of patients to risk. The Committ ee observes that merely circulating the 
guidelines to the State Governments with no means to ensure their strict compliance 
would not serve any purpose. There need s to be a component of surprise  
inspections in the existing monitoring mechanism  This, the Committee believes, 
would go a long way in ensuring compliance to the guidelines and act as a deterrent 
against violations by the defaulting agencies. 
 
9.9 One of the important initiatives under the National Programme for Control of 
Blindness is collection of eyes donated for corneal implantation. The Department has 
reported gradual improvement in the donation of eyes.  From 28,007 eyes collected 
during 2005-06, number of eyes collected during 2008-09 reached 41,780.  
 
9.10 The Committee would however, like to point out that achievement level still 
r e m a i n s  l e s s  t h a n  t h e  t a r g e t .   W h a t  i s  m o r e  d i s t u r b i n g  i s  t h a t  t h e r e  i s  a n  a c t u a l  
requirement of at least 80,000 to 1,00,000 of corneas per year in the country for 
transplantation purpose.  The Committee is  of the view that besides lack of 
awareness, other major cause for low level of eye donation/collection is that 
infrastructure required for the purpose is  simply not there at the ground level.

out that as per the above st ate-specific details, percentage of corneas utilized is 
much below the accepted 30 to 40 percent level.  One obvious reason for such a low 
level of utilization can be the upkeep and maintenance of Eye Banks being not as 
per the standard norms. The Committee , therefore, recommends to the Department 
to keep the activities of all the entities/institutions  involved in providing eye 
collection activities under constant scruti ny/monitoring and take urgent remedial 
steps so that the stumbling blocks in the optimum utilization of eyes collected is 
achieved by more number of beneficiaries getting back their vision. 
 The Committee would like to know as to whether the Government is aware 
of the work done by Dr. Sinha.  
 
X. NATIONAL IODINE DEFICIENCY DISORDERS CONTROL 
PROGRAMME 
 
10.1 Deficiency of iodine can cause physical and mental retardation, cretinism, 
abortions, still-birth, deaf, mutism, squint and various types of goiter. Out of 325 districts 

 130
covering all the states and Union Territories where sample surveys were conducted, 263 
districts have been found to be endemic, where the prevelance of iodine deficiency 
disorder is more than 10 per cent and es timated 71 million people are suffering from 
Iodine Deficiency Disorders.  
10.2 The budget allocation under the Programme for 2009-10 was Rs. 40 crore which 
was reduced at the RE stage to Rs 29 crore. The Department has informed that uptill 
28.2.10, the fund utilization was to the tune of  Rs 15.32 crore.  On being asked regarding 
the drastic reduction in the allocation at RE 2009-10, the Department has informed that 
the reduced allocation was mainly due to review of the technical specifications of salt 
testing kits, and approval of Empowered Programme Committee (EPC) being under 
consideration. 
 
  10.3 The Committee is not very happy with the trends of fund utilization under 
the programme. During 2007-08 and 2008-09 against an allocation of Rs. 25.00 crore 
and Rs. 32.00 crore respectively, expenditur e reported is only Rs. 19.45 crore and 
Rs. 17.95 crore respectively.   The Committee observes that every year there 
have been substantial amount of savi ngs over the allocated funds. The Committee 
notes with disappointment that this neit her speaks of good financial management 
nor good programme implementation und er the Programme. The Committee once 
again recommends to the Department to devi se measures to overcome the obstacles 
coming in the way of proper implementation of the programme initiatives.  
10.4 The Committee hopes that the Empowered Programme Committee (EPC) 
approval must have been received by now and also the biding process on the 
technical specifications of salt testin g kits, must have been completed. The 
Committee would like to emphasise that all conceivable efforts may be made so as to 
ensure optimal and judicious allocation of funds in active coordination with state 
authority for timely achievements of the targets during 2010-11.  
 
10.5 The committee notes that as per the surveys that were conducted by the 
Directorate General of Health Services, Indian Council of Medical Research, the State 
Health Directorates and Health Institutes, ou t of 324 districts in all the 28 States and 7 
Union Territories, 263 districts are endemic. The proportion of districts endemic for 
Iodine Deficiency Disorders out of total districts surveyed so far were found to be more 
in Bihar, Himachal Pradesh, Jammu & Kashmir, Madhya Pradesh, Uttarakhand, 

 131
Andaman & Nicobar Islands, Chandigarh, Da man & Diu, Dadra & Nagar Haveli, as 
compared to other State/UTs. 
 
10.6 The Committee understands from the above  that as many as nine States/ UTs 
have been highly endemic iodine deficien cy. The Committee, however, does not find 
that any focused intervention has been initiated in the above States/ UTs to reduce 
the deficiency rate. Since, the above ob servation is a result of certain sample 
districts surveyed in the above States/ UTs the numbers would be much more if all 
the districts in these States/ UTs are co vered. Therefore, the Committee suggests 
that a special intervention needs to be made in all of the above States/UTs.  
Intensified IEC activities may also be carr ied out covering all the districts so that 
the prevalence rate comes to less than 10. The Committee would like the 
Department to furnish details of the samp le survey report concerning all the States/ 
UTs covered for its perusal.   
 
XI INTEGRATED DISEASE SURVEILLANCE PROGRAMME 
11.1 The objectives of the Integrated Diseas e Surveillance Programme are to establish 
a decentralized state based system of surveillance for communicable and non-
communicable diseases and to improve the efficiency of the existing surveillance 
activities of disease control programmes. Under the Programme, for 2009-10, Rs 45.50 
crore were allocated. As per the information furnished by the Ministry till 12
th March, 
2010 an expenditure of Rs 35.98 crore were incurred.  The Committee, however, notes 
that allocated funds during 2007-08 and 2008-09  have remained under-utilized.  
11.2 The Committee is disappointed at the achievements stated against the targets set 
for 2009-10. The Committee has been given to understand that 610 District surveillance 
teams (cumulative since beginning of project including 202 teams of phase – III) were to 
be trained during 2008-09 and 2009-10. The Committee finds that though 136 Dist. 
Surveillance Officers were trained in two week long FETD programme and District 
Surveillance Teams of all States/UTs covered except Bihar, Jharkhand, D& N Haveli, 
Daman & Diu & Lakshadweep were trained  during 2008-09, there is no clear 
information regarding the status during 2009-10 except that the training is underway. 
Similarly, against the 800 sites (State HQ,  District HQ, Govt. medical Colleges, 
Important Institutes) targeted to be connected through Broad band network, during 2008-
09 and 2009-10, Broad Band network was instal led across 606 districts during 2008-09, 

 132
while the only information furnished on th e progress during 2009-10 by the Ministry is 
that connectivity on Broad Band network is being established through National 
Informatics Network. Under the head of data collection and analysis, though some 
information regarding the data collected and analysed from phase I, II and III states is 
available for 2008-09, no information is available in this regard during 2009-10.  
11.3 The Committee fails to understand as to why clear information has not been 
furnished by the Ministry against the targ ets set by it for 2009-10. The Committee 
observes that concrete data is necessary for proper assessment of the targets set and 
merely stating that  training programme is in progress or connectivity on Broad 
Band is being established without quantify ing the progress made evinces ignorance 
of the most basic principles of appraisa l on the part of the Department.  The 
Committee would like the Department to furnish details of achievement during 
2009-10 regarding the targets mentioned above in the table for 2009-10 while 
furnishing its ATN and also be more circumspect while furnishing the information 
in the Outcome Budget.  
 
11.4 Regarding the query as to whether the required trained manpower is available for 
the successful implementation of this Programme, and if not the action plan in this 
regard, the Department has informed that training for PHC doctors, Lab technicians, 
other paramedical staff, etc. has been completed in Phase I and II States and is being 
undertaken in Phase III States. The Department  has also stated that a total of 766 
Technical Consultants are to be recruited (1 Epidemiologist each at State Surveillance 
Unit (SSU) and District Surveillance Unit (DSU); 1 Microbiologist each at SSU and 50 
priority District Labs; 1 Entomologist each at all SSUs), of these, 261 Epidemiologists, 
29 Microbiologists and 10 Entomologists have joined State / District Surveillance units 
for assisting the State/ District health officers. Training of these newly recruited health 
professionals has also been undertaken. The 2
nd round of recruitment of technical 
consultants for 466 remaining vacant posts will be done after getting the approval for 
restructuring and extension of IDSP.  
 
11.5 The Committee is happy to note that  the recruitment and training of the 
required manpower under IDSP is proceeding at a positive pace. The Committee 
observes that the presence of trained surveillance manpower are key to the 
prevention and control of diseases/ epidem ic in such a large country as India. 

 133
Hence, the Committee hopes that the targ ets of recruiting and training technical 
consultants for the remaining 400 vacant post would have been completed after 
due approval for restructuring and extension of IDSP were obtained. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends the Departme nt to furnish the details in this 
regard for its perusal. The Committee wo uld also like to be apprised of the 
progress made towards establishing video conferencing at the remaining sites and 
strengthening the remaining priority labs.

OBSERVATIONS/  RECOMMENDATIONS   AT A GLANCE 
 
PART-A HEALTH SECTOR 
II   BUDGETARY ALLOCATION 
 
With this trend of allocation of f unds and utilization thereof, the Committee 
does not have much hope about optimum release of funds out of the approved outlay 
of Rs. 41092.92 crore in the remaining two years of the Eleventh Plan.  The Department 
will have to make concerted efforts so as to ensure optimum utilization of plan 
allocation of Rs. 5560.00 crore in 2010-11.  On ly then, there are chances of enhanced 
provision being made in 2011-12, the last year of the Eleventh Plan.                   (Para 2.4) 
The Committee in its 27
th Report on Demands for Grants (2008-09) had 
commented on the Department’s inability to project its budgetary requirements in an 
effective way for the simple reason that the utilization of funds during the Tenth Plan 
was far from satisfactory. The Committee finds that the same trend continues in the 
Eleventh Plan period also. During 2007-08 a nd 2009-10,  keeping in view the pace of 
expenditure, allocated funds were substantiall y reduced at the RE stage. What is more 
worrisome is that the Department could not achieve optimum utilization of this 
reduced allocation during these two years.  Enhanced Plan allocation of Rs. 4450.00 in 
2009-10 failed to serve the purpose as it had to be brought down to Rs. 3825.25 crore, 
marginally lower than the 2008-09 allocation of Rs. 3650.00 crore.  Utilization status in 
2009-10 was more disappointing.  As on 28
th February, 2010, expenditure reported was 
Rs. 2559.61 crore which is less than the utilization of Rs. 3008.40 crore in the previous 
year. The Committee also takes note of th e fact that expenditure reported upto 31
st 
January 2010 under Plan head was only Rs. 2034. 06 crore. This clearly points to the fact 
that major utilization is taking place in th e last quarter of the financial year, thus 
indicating non-adherence to fiscal discipline.              (Para 2.5) 
 
 The Committee’s examination of the following major heads of account has 
revealed recurrent and persistent sub-optimal utilization of budgetary provisions 
(Plan) during the Eleventh Plan period:-

  (Para 2.6) 
 
 The Committee is dismayed at the fact  that its continuous concerns and 
recommendations to the Department to put a proper mechanism in place for - 
presenting its case for fund requirement before the Planning Commission  in a 
realistic manner, and to arrest the trend of under utilization of funds seem to have 
failed to make any impact.  Atleast the Department needs to try some innovative 
methods to achieve optimum utilization of funds. Health is a crucial sector for the 
allround development of the nation and accordingly the Committee has been 
advocating higher allocation of funds for this sector every year.  But with the 
Department itself admitting to the slow pace of expenditure in respect of many 
schemes/programmes initiated by it, the Committee finds advocacy for additional 
funds serving no purpose. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Department 
to undertake a comprehensive analysis of th e trend of expenditure for the past three 
years with a view to identifying the underlying causes of underutilization. Such an 
exercise should enable the Department to no t only improve the utilization level of the 
budgeted funds but also to improve upon the existing system of assessing requirement 
of funds. The Committee is also of the opinion that the Department needs to take up 
with the implementing agencies as and when schemes are found not being 
implemented as per the Action Plan.                (Para 2.7) 
The Committee desires to be apprised of the necessary action initiated and 
pursued by the Department to streamline the formulation of budgetary requirements 

 136
in a realistic fashion and also to expedite necessary approvals for operationalization of 
various formulated schemes/projects.                                                    (Para 2.8) 
The Committee is specially concerned with the slow pace of implementation of 
schemes like Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Sur aksha Yojna (PMSSY), Cancer Research, 
National Mental Health Programme, Assistance for Capacity Building for Trauma 
Centers, Oversight Committee, Upgradat ion/strengthening of Nursing Services, 
Strengthening/Upgradation of Pharmacy schools/colleges, Strengthening/creation of 
paramedical institutions as well as underutilization of allocated funds by some of the 
Hospitals/institutions. The Committee observes that this recurrent and persistent 
trend of underutilization is indicative of ineffectiveness of monitoring of 
implementation of schemes and utilization of funds.  The Committee therefore 
recommends to the Department to keep the activities of these schemes/institutions 
under constant scrutiny/monitoring and take urgent remedial steps so that the 
stumbling blocks in the optimum utilization of allocated/sanctioned funds are 
removed.                                       (Para 2.9) 
Committee’s attention has also been drawn by unspent balances lying with a 
number of Institutions.  As on 1
st December, 2009, maximum unspent balances 
amounting to Rs. 28.79 crore, Rs. 36.52 cror e and Rs. 80.43 crore were reported against 
the North-Eastern Indira Gandhi Regional Institute of Health and Medical Sciences, 
Shillong, LGB Regional Institute of Ment al Health, Tezpur, Assam and AIIMS New 
Delhi. Except AIIMS, the other two Institutes had unspent balances as on 1 st April, 
2009.  Reasons for availability of such a huge amount of unspent balances are not 
known to the Committee.  However, such a st ate of affairs in national level institutes 
is indeed a cause of concern for the Committ ee.  This clearly shows that some major 
activities both ongoing/proposed might be  adversely affected.  The Committee would 
appreciate if the matter is taken up with  the concerned authorities without further 
delay.                             (Para 2.10) 
The Committee takes note of the fact that the Department has taken remedial 
measures like for non-recurring grants, no fresh releases are being authorized unless 
audited Utilization Certificate for grant released in the year prior to the last year (i.e. 
with a ‘grace period’ of one year) has been received, subject to the condition that the 

 137
sanction for the earlier grant does not permit a longer period of utilization. Further, the 
provisions of the General Financial Rules, which require that in case of recurring 
grants, funds for the same purpose beyond 75% of the next year’s provision will not be 
released unless audited Utilization Certificate for the grants released in the previous 
year is received, are being strictly implemented. Impact of such measures does not 
seem to be very effective as number of pending UCs continues to remain very high.  
Not only this, such a trend also indicates that schemes are not being implemented as 
envisaged.                  (Para 2.12) 
The Committee notes that as a further effort to maximize liquidation of UCs, a 
communication has been sent by the Secr etary (Health) to the Principal Health 
Secretaries of all the States. If need be, th is exercise needs to be pursued further with 
higher authorities.                                                                        (Para 2.13) 
 
The Committee appreciates this forward movement w.r.t. audit of Autonomous 
Bodies/Societies/Grantee Institutions/Programmes receiving Grants-in-Aid from the 
Department. The Committee recommends to the Department to furnish the major 
audit findings of 2008-09 and 2009-10 and actio n taken thereon. A list of Autonomous 
Bodies/Societies/Grantee Institutions/Programmes likely to be covered during 2010-11 
may also be furnished to the Committee.                                                                 (Para 2.15) 
 
The Committee notes that the irregularities pointed out by the Internal Audit 
Wing in its audit paras concerning NVBDCP, Delhi, NIMHANS, Bangalore, ICMR, 
New Delhi and V.P. Chest Institute, Delhi University (for two paras 24 & 28) are yet to 
be settled.  The Committee would like to be apprised of the updated status in this 
regard.                                                                                                                              (Para 2.16) 
 The Committee has been keeping a clos e watch on the level of pendency of 
Audit paras contained in CAG Report on matters relating to the Department. The 
Committee observes that around 60 paras are under various stages of pendency and 
some of them date back to the period 1998-2000. Committee’s attention has been 
especially drawn by the following pending Audit paras:

 139
III CENTRAL GOVERNMENT HEALTH SCHEME (CGHS) 
 
The Committee is unhappy to note the continuous underutilization of Plan 
funds during the first three financial years of the Eleventh  Plan, more so when the 
Department had sought an increase of funds during the RE stage during 2008-09 and 
2009-10 and it could not even utilize the funds provided to it at the BE stage. The 
Committee, therefore, strongly feels that the Department has to justify the need for 
additional funds by ensuring optimum utilization thereof and be careful in 
placing/proposing its revised estimate so that funds are not unnecessarily blocked and 
go unspent.                                                                                                                        (Para 3.3) 
The Committee does not find any logic for under provisioning for CGHS under 
Materials & Supplies as also for IT activ ities Head which are bound to affect the 
quality of services available to the CGHS beneficiaries. The Committee also observes 
that total tentative pendency with regard to medical claims of pensioners as on 
January, 2010 is Rs. 200 crore, including bill s pertaining to 2008-09.  In such a scenario, 
allocation of Rs. 377.87 crore for pensioners  against the projected requirement of Rs. 
627.00 crore for 2010-11 is bound to increase th e pendency further.  The Committee is 
of the view that full allocation of required funds has to be there so that pensioners are 
not made to face uncalled for hardships.                                                                    (Para 3.5) 
 The Committee is happy to note that a number of initiatives under CGHS are 
proposed to be undertaken during 2010-11. On e such initiative is expansion of a pilot 
project for Preventive Health Check-up for pensioners and serving employees over the 
age of 40 years at CGHS dispensary, Sect or-12 and Sector-4, R.K. Puram, New Delhi . 
The Committee would like the Department to share its experiences of the pilot project 
and recommends to the Department to expand the initiative to more dispensaries in a 
time-bound manner.                                                      (Para 3.6) 
 The Committee also welcomes the introd uction of a proposal under which the 
empanelled hospitals will forward the bill for the treatment of pensioner CGHS 
beneficiaries to UTI-TSL and payment of the same within 10 days.  Such an initiative 
is bound to help the pensioners in availing of healthcare without any hindrance.  
                       (Para 3.7) 

 140
The Committee views the introduction of the current proposal of the Ministry 
with serious concern. The Committee is no t convinced by Department’s categorical 
statement that it does not intend to replace the CGHS with this new insurance scheme. 
It is amply clear that the scheme would be mandatory for future employees and 
pensioners.  The Committee would like to point out that CGHS is one of the very 
important social security measures which ha s been providing health care services at 
economical rates to its beneficiaries for so many decades. The Committee fails to 
comprehend the rationale for depriving all the beneficiaries of such an essential 
service in the long run.  The Committee would like to point out that there is a well-
structured set up of CGHS dispensaries, Govt. Hospitals providing allopathic and 
AYUSH healthcare and also a number of empanelled diagnostic centres and private 
hospitals.  In the recent years a number of initiatives have also been taken for 
strengthening/revamping different comp onents under the Scheme.  A number of 
additional dispensaries are also proposed to be set up in the near future.  The existing 
CGHS scheme is part of the social responsibility of Govt. for its serving/retired 
employees. Full reimbursement as per th e prescribed norms is payable to the 
beneficiaries. There is no upper limit on annual expenditure on the treatment of a 
beneficiary.  The very idea of capping the amount of coverage to Rs. 5 lakh per family 
per year as well as the limit of coverage to only - Self, spouse, two dependent children 
and dependent parents and thereby forcing for payment of the fixed percentage of 
premium per additional dependent family member under the proposed scheme 
indicates that the Govt. intends to dilute its social obligation primarily to its 
employees. This is unacceptable to the Committee.                         (Para 3.9) 
The Committee is dismayed at the Departme nt’s approach from the very outset.  
First, it had taken the plea of a large number of private hospitals and diagnostic centres 
empanelled under the CGHS and the opposition  from the staff side to any such move. 
Then, subsequent to Committee’s recommendation that flexible timings are order of 
the day and  desirable in the interest of many of the CGHS beneficiaries who are 
unable to avail the facilities of dispensaries in the morning hours, the Department in 
its Action Taken Note on Committee’s 27
th Report had stated that the Ministry intends 
to take the matter before the Committee of Secretaries. The latest reply proves beyond 
doubt that the Department is not willing to move forward in the matter. Like in the 

 141
previous instances, the new plea taken by the Department is its inability to go ahead 
on account of its existing staff strength.  The Committee is of the opinion that if there 
is shortage of manpower, necessary action plan may be drawn up and approval sought 
for requisite manpower at appropriate level. The Committee would like to reiterate 
that the matter needs to be taken up seriou sly and in the right spirit.  The Committee 
would like to be informed of the progress made in this regard.         (Para 3.11) 
 
 The process of computerization under the CGHS which was initiated during 
2005 is continuing. The Committee notes that finally the Department has been able to 
finish the work of computerization in six cities i.e. Delhi, Kolkata, Nagpur, Pune, 
Chennai and Ranchi and it is nearing comp letion in Hyderabad, Mumbai and Patna. 
However, the Committee is constrained to no te that the Department has not been able 
to add momentum to the computerization wo rk even after the expiry of about five 
years as 15 cities still remain to be covere d. The Committee therefore recommends that 
the computerization work in the remaining cities be completed in a mission mode by 
the end of the Eleventh Plan.              (Para 3.12) 
 
 The Committee appreciates the steps taken by the Department to neutralize the 
teething problems afflicting the smooth implementation of the computerization. 
However, the ground realities are not much encouraging. The Committee is informed 
that there is shortage of computer operat ors and the doctors have to first see the 
patients and then feed the relevant data in the computer system leading to delays and 
the resultant long queues. This way the computerization has only been able to help 
keep a check on the inventory in the store room and save time in putting up request 
for local purchases wherever required. Howe ver, it has not been able to obviate the 
problems faced by the beneficiaries particularly the old age pensioners who have to 
stand for long hours in queue. The Committee,  therefore, recommends that adequate 
trained manpower may be made available at the earliest.                     (Para 3.14) 
 
3.16 The Committee is happy to note that after much delay the Department has 
finally been able to open a dispensary each in NOIDA and Dwarka. However, as the 
number of CGHS beneficiaries is very la rge in that area, the Committee recommends 

 142
to the Department to put all its acts togeth er to start the three dispensaries for which 
plots have been purchased in Dwarka and one dispensary at Gurgaon. 
                  (Para 3.16) 
 The Committee is given to understand that the workload on the dispensaries in 
Delhi has increased manifold. The Committee therefore recommends to the Department 
to have a realistic assessment of workload in CGHS dispensaries in Delhi with a view 
to explore opening up of new dispensaries to mitigate the problems faced by the 
beneficiaries. The Committee would recomm end to the Department to furnish a 
detailed status note on the no. of beneficiaries attached with each dispensary, zone-
wise in Delhi and the NCR and plans of the Department to open new dispensaries in 
underserved areas of Delhi and NCR. The Committee may also be briefed about the 
availability of unused land lying with CGHS in different cities covered by CGHS 
alongwith the purpose of their acquirement.            (Para 3.17) 
 
 The Committee is not very happy with the element of adhocism in such a 
crucial area. The Committee would like to point out that the number of vacancies in a 
particular grade are bound to put pressure on the existing workforce thereby 
compromising with the quality of services provided to the beneficiaries. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends to the Department to urgently chalk out a strategy 
to fill up the vacant positions in all grades under CGHS in a time-bound manner.  
                  (Para 3.21) 
 
 The Committee has noticed substantial variation in approved rates of 
investigations / treatment procedures in various metro cities. The Committee does not 
find much merit in the argument of the Department that rates offered by the private 
hospitals and diagnostic centres depend upon the prevailing market rates, number and 
business volume from CGHS beneficiarie s likely to be derived by eligible 
institutions/private institutions, which vary from city to city. The Committee is of the 
opinion that substantial variation of 50 to 100 per cent in approved rates and that too in 
cities like Delhi, Mumbai, Chennai and Kolk ata cannot be considered commensurate 
with the variation in economic dynamics. The Committee accordingly, recommends to 

 143
the Department to come up with a strategy to  counter this variation of rates by having 
minimum disparities in the rates.                        (Para 3.23) 
  
 Though the Committee appreciates Department’s initiative for ensuring quality 
service to CGHS beneficiaries and has no doubts over the professionalism of 
NABH/NABL, yet it does not find much merit in making it compulsory as a 
precondition for empanelment under C GHS. The NABL/NABH lay down certain 
norms and standards to be observed and on inspection, they provide accreditation to 
those who fulfill the said standards. There is no guarantee that once accredited, the 
Hospital/diagnostic centre will maintain those standards till the next inspection by 
NABL/NABH. Further, it has been observed that many of the reputed hospitals don’t 
go for accreditation under them. In such a scenario it may be counterproductive as the 
beneficiaries may be deprived of services  under such reputed Hospitals/diagnostic 
centres. The Committee accordingly recommends to the Department to be cautious on 
this move.                             (Para 3.25) 
 
 The Committee would also like to have  information regarding the number of 
NABH/NABL accredited Govt. hospitals/diagnostic centres as well as private 
hospitals/diagnostic centres empanelled under CGHS.                     (Para 3.26) 
 
 The Committee would like to point out that prior to 2001, all the premier 
specialized Hospitals had opted for CGHS empanelment.  The Committee wonders as 
to whether the recently empanelled Hospital of Delhi can provide the same services as 
being provided by Rajiv Gandhi Cancer Ho spital or Dharamshila Cancer Hospital.  
The Committee is also not aware about the quality status of the only empanelled 
Hospital.  The Committee reiterates that the kind of specialized services being offered 
by a particular hospital, specially for terminal diseases like cancer and not the lowest 
rates should be the basic criteria for selection of a hospital.  One must not forget that 
the main objective of CGHS is to provide qu ality health care at economical rates. Not 
only this, Government is supposed to reimbu rse only the packages rates.  Payment of 
difference in treatment expenditure is the co ncern of the beneficiary.  In Committee’s 
view, package rates need to be governed by quality service.  Nobody stops the 

 144
concerned authorities to negotiate with the applicant hospitals for arriving at mutually 
acceptable package rates. It cannot be that services offered by a small hospital can be 
considered on an equal footing with an advanced well established hospital.  The 
Committee, accordingly, recommends framing of a policy for revision of rates after 
every two years to realistically fix the ra tes of treatment. The Committee may be 
informed about the action taken by the Department in this regard.        (Para 3.28) 
 
 The Committee appreciates the number of initiatives taken by the Department 
for effective functioning of CGHS. The Committee however, feels that wide publicity 
through local and print media needs to be given in this regard.  Only then CGHS 
beneficiaries will be in a position to avail of such facilities.                     (Para 3.30)
   
IV SAFDARJUNG HOSPITAL AND VARDHMAN MAHAVEER MEDICAL 
COLLEGE, NEW DELHI 
 
 The Committee notes that against projected Plan requirement of Rs. 180.60 crore 
for 2010-11 for the Hospital, allocation of Rs. 118.60 crore has been made. Given the 
fact that out of the RE plan allocation of Rs. 129.92 crore for 2009-10, the Hospital was 
able to spend Rs. 112.76 crore upto 28
th February, 2010, with every possibility of full 
utilization by the end of the year, the Committee is unable to comprehend the reasons 
behind a huge gap of Rs. 62.00 crore between the projected and budgeted funds for 
2010-11.  The Committee, therefore, recommends that additional funds be allocated for 
the Hospital at RE stage so that lack of funds should not hamper the re-developmental 
activities undertaken by the Hospital.                         (Para 4.2) 
 
 The Committee is very concerned with the vacancy position in different 
categories of posts at the Hospital. This coupled with the admission of the head of the 
Hospital itself that the sanctioned strength is far below the practical requirement, 
presents a sorry state of affairs. The Co mmittee is of the opinion that vacancies 
irrespective of any category are not only directly or indirectly bound to have an 
adverse impact on the quantity and quality of the services rendered by the Hospital 
but also result in overstressing of the existing staff. Further, the vacancies also have 
the potential to affect the quality of education imparted at the attached medical 

 145
college. The Committee is given to understand that in case of Resident Doctors, 
vacancies occur frequently and are filled regularly depending on the requirement. The 
Committee is, however, not able to comprehend as to why there is about 25% of 
vacancies at any given point of time. At least the Department can take pre-emptive 
action so that it has the next batch of Reside nt Doctors available to  step-in in place of 
the outgoing Resident Doctors. Similarly, vacancies in Group A and Group B Gazetted 
posts are far above the justifiable limits.  The Committee accordingly recommends to 
the Department to take up the issue of vacancies seriously and on priority basis. It also 
recommends a review of the requirement of manpower vis-à-vis the sanctioned 
strength of the Hospital and college. The Committee would like to be informed about 
the progress made in this regard.                         (Para 4.4) 
 Committee’s attention was also drawn towa rds acute shortage of  nursing staff at 
the Hospital. Non-availability of good accommodation for nursing staff was cited as the 
main factor responsible for such  a situation.  Existing staff qu arters at Srinivaspuri were 
considered to be too inadequate.  The Comm ittee was given to understand that another 
project was coming up at Dwarka. The Committee is of the view that provision of 
accommodation nearest to the Hospital is the basic requirement for all hospital 
employees, especially for the nurses rendering 24 hour services.  The Committee, 
accordingly, recommends that provision of required accommodation facilities for 
nurses should be taken up on priority basis.  Viability of such facility at the Hospital 
premises itself under the ongoing Redevelopm ent Plan of the Hospital needs to be 
looked into.                             (Para 4.5) 
 The Committee is unhappy with the pace with which things are moving in 
respect of the Re-development Plan of the Hospital. The Committee in its 27
th Report 
had urged the Department to make wholeh earted efforts to expedite the procedural 
formalities regarding this. The Committee notes that the Department itself has now 
stated a timeline of six years for the entire work to complete. Keeping in view the 
inordinate delay already made since its inclusion in the Eleventh Plan, the Committee 
recommends to the Department to make all conceivable efforts so that the whole 
redevelopment work is completed within this period of six years. For this to happen, 
targets need to be set, resources placed  at the disposal in time and implementing 

 146
agencies need to be made accountable to  accomplish the set targets from the very 
outset.                                                     (Para 4.7) 
 The Committee is extremely disappointe d with the persisting slow progress 
made by the Department w.r.t. setting up of the Renal Transplantation Unit and IVF 
Unit. The Committee recommends to the Department to remove all bottlenecks in 
setting up of the two units at the earliest. The Committee would like to have a status 
note on the same inter-alia containing details regarding the original timeline set, 
reasons for inordinate delay, resultant cost escalation and fresh timeline set with 
special efforts taken to meet the timeline this time.                       (Para 4.10) 
 
 The Committee is constrained to observe that the allocated funds for 2009-10 
could not be utilised even after steep reduction at the RE stage. To add to this the 
Department’s reply that the reduction was due  to realistic assessment of the needs of 
the Institute, proves that the earlier estimate, at the BE stage was not proper. Moreover, 
the fact that the College could not utilize this reduced amount also makes it clear that 
there is some element of improper planning at some stage. The Committee, 
accordingly, recommends to the Department to have a realistic assessment of the fund 
requirement for the College at the outset itself.                     (Para 4.12) 
 
 V DR. RAM MANOHAR LOHIA HOSPTIAL, NEW DELHI 
 
 The Committee takes note of the fact that inspite of a lot of developmental work 
being undertaken by the Hospital, several of the items of work like setting up of the 
Renal Transplantation Unit have gone beyond the targeted time-line.  Concerned that 
time-overruns are certain to result in cost overruns, the Committee recommends that 
the Hospital chalk out a time-bound Action Plan for such projects. The Committee 
would like to point out that the emphasis should not only be on the optimal 
utilization but also on the judicious utiliz ation of scarce funds. The Committee at the 
same time recommends to the Department to ensure that the developmental activities 
initiated by the Hospital do not get hampered due to scarcity of funds.           (Para 5.4) 
 
 The Committee welcomes the proposal to start a general maternity ward, 
construction of a New Casualty Building, multi-storey super speciality block and 

 147
setting up of Centre of Excellence for Mental Health. However, the Committee 
recommends to the Department to keep a close watch on the progress of new 
initiatives from the outset so that they are accomplished as targeted. The Committee 
also recommends to the Department to take  all necessary steps for early possession of 
land in the G point near Trauma Care Cent re. The Committee may be informed of the 
progress made w.r.t. the new initiatives.                         (Para 5.5) 
 
 At the outset the Committee places its displeasure over the mismatch in the 
figures provided to it with respect to vacant posts vis-à-vis ‘sanctioned and in-position’ 
posts.  For example, in respect of Group ‘A’ CHS posts, sanctioned and in-position 
posts are 219 and 194 (including 20 contractua l posts) respectively while vacancies 
shown are 27.  Such variations exist in respec t of some other categories of posts also.  
The Committee would like the Department to explain this mismatch. The Committee 
is constrained to find a lot of vacancies across the grades. The Hospital is already 
overburdened with patient load and in such a scenario it is really a matter of concern 
that such a large number of vacancies are allowed to continue. The Committee is of the 
opinion that in the smooth functioning of the hospital services, all the posts whether 
they are in technical side, nursing side or in the clerical side, have their part to play. 
Vacancies beyond permissible limits in an y grade affect the overall patient care 
offered by the Hospital as well as resu lt in overburdening the existing set of 
employees. Taking into account the increased patient load, the Committee 
recommends to the Department to take immediate steps for reassessment of the 
manpower requirement in the Hospital and fill up the vacant posts in the various 
grades at the earliest. The Committee may be apprised of the steps initiated by the 
Department to streamline the existing procedure of recruitment so that vacancies in 
the different grades are filled up at the earliest.                       (Para 5.7) 
 
 
VI ALL INDIA INSTITUTE OF MEDICAL SCIENCES (AIIMS) 
  
The Committee is anguished to note that out of a total allocation of Rs. 1461 
crore for the Eleventh Plan, the Institute do es not seem to be able to utilize the 
earmarked funds for the Plan period during the first three years of the Plan period. 

 148
The Institute has been able to utilize on ly Rs. 648.00 crore (as on 28-2-2010).  The 
Committee sees little hope for optimum utiliz ation of remaining funds during the last 
two years of Eleventh Plan. As a result, the initiatives/ongoing projects are not likely 
to be executed as targeted originally. Th e Committee observes that as many as 21 
projects were envisaged to be completed dur ing the Eleventh Plan for which an outlay 
of Rs. 623.63 crore was made. The Committee is, however, dismayed to note that by the 
end of the first three years of the Plan, utilization reported is only Rs. 70.18 crore. The 
situation is worsened by the fact that work in respect of as many as 14 projects is yet to 
start. These projects include expansio n of OPD Block and New Emergency Ward, 
Burns Unit at JPN Apex Trauma Centre, Multi-level parking, Pediatrics Surgery. Out 
of total expenditure of Rs. 70.18 crore, Rs. 55.00 crore has been utilized for minor works 
in Hospital/ hostels/ residential areas of the campus. Thus, progress made in the 
remaining six projects can also be considered at the initial stage only. The Committee 
cannot remain a silent spectator to present circumstances as AIIMS is India’s premier 
Institute providing specialized health services to patients from across the country. The 
Committee is of the firm view that it is  high time that an effective monitoring 
mechanism is set in motion.                                      (Para 6.5) 
 
  The Committee takes note of the fact that AIIMS has initiated the process 
of making a composite Redevelopment Plan of its Masjid Moth, Ansari Nagar and 
A.V. Nagar campuses. The work of prepara tion of redevelopment plan of A.V. Nagar 
campus has been entrusted to CPWD. The work of Ansari Nagar and Masjid Moth 
campuses has been entrusted to HSCC by the Governing Body on 23
rd January, 2009. 
The concept Master Plan was finally a pproved by the Proj ect Implementation 
Committee on 12 th May, 2009. The revised Master Plan as presented before the 
Institute Body in December, 2009 has since been submitted to NDMC for approval. 
HSCC has also been entrusted with the co nsultancy and project management work of 
the redevelopment of old OPD Block.                                     (Para 6.7) 
 
 While appreciating the candid statement of the Health Secretary, the Committee 
fails to comprehend the basis for such a Company being allowed to be associated with 
carrying out tasks of such magnitude and of public interest. The Committee 

 149
understands that a number of projects in different areas are being handled by HSCC at 
present. The Committee recommends that a St atus Note giving all the details of the 
various projects awarded to HSCC may be furnished to it at the earliest. The 
Department should take the issue very seriously and if need be, some kind of penalty 
clause may be introduced in the agreement itself to fix liability for any delay. The 
Committee may be apprised of the development in this regard.                      (Para 6.9) 
 
 Committee’s attention has been drawn by a very disturbing trend of utilization 
of allocated funds by AIIMS. Director, AIIMS during his deposition before the 
Committee, informed that during 2008-09 and 2009-10 some Plan Funds were diverted 
to Non-plan for paying the Sixth Pay commission’s arrears and various other 
allowances to AIIMS employees. As a result,  development projects could not be taken 
up. Frequent changes in the plan projects also had a negative impact . The Committee 
would like to point out that diversion of plan funds to non-plan side is in direct 
contravention of fiscal norms. The Committee deplores such a development at the 
premier Institute of the country. It seems that this trend has been continuing since 
long. The Committee wonders about the role of Internal Audit Wing and Financial 
Adviser of the Department. The Committee is also very unhappy with the non-
provision of required non-plan funds meant for payment of arrears and allowances to 
the employees which are anticipated beforehand. The Committee would appreciate if 
fiscal management at AIIMS is brought to  order. The Committee cannot accept the 
diversion of plan funds meant for development projects to non-plan side. Urgent 
action needs to be, taken in this crucial ma tter so that this trend is not allowed to 
continue any further.                                                                                                     (Para 6.10) 
 
 The Committee is happy to note that though inordinately delayed, finally there 
is some concrete action on the recommendations of the Valiathan Committee Report. 
The Committee would, however, recommend full implementation of the 
recommendations at an early date. The Co mmittee also hopes th at the process of 
examination of recommendations requiring  statutory amendments by the High 
Powered Committee will be completed at the earliest and implemented without 
unnecessary delay.                                                  (Para 6.13) 

 150
 The Committee is unhappy with the dilly-dallying approach of the Department 
on the issue of setting up of the Burns Unit. The Committee notes with displeasure 
Department’s attitude from the outset whic h has been evident from the delay in the 
matter. ‘Avoiding duplication’ can never be  considered as a valid justification for 
dropping this project. The Committee notes th at funds to the tune of Rs. 48 crore have 
been earmarked for this purpose during the Eleventh Plan. The Committee is unaware 
whether the Department, before taking a view in the matter, has taken into account the 
patient load in the Burns Unit at the Safdarjung Hospital. Further, the Committee 
would also like to point out that availability of any service at Safdarjung Hospital 
cannot be made the criteria for not initiating the same at AIIMS. If that be so, the same 
argument can be put forth for not adding a particular service at Safdarjung Hospital 
since the same was available at the next door AIIMS.  The Committee would like to 
point out that with the ever increasing patient load, both the hospitals have been 
providing exemplary services. Any addition or expansion of service will be going to 
provide better healthcare to our people.                                                         (Para 6.15)
     
 The Committee is pained by the acute shortage of the manpower at the Institute 
and is of the opinion that the same is not justifiable in any manner. The Committee 
applauds the existing set of employees of the Institute, who inspite of the tremendous 
work pressure, have been able to uphold the high standards and reputation of the 
Institute. With large number of vacancies at Faculty level posts, it is practically 
difficult to maintain the high standards of patient care and teaching in the Institute for 
which it has been known. The Committee accordingly recommends to the Department 
to make all out efforts to fill up the vacant  posts – both faculty and non-faculty and all 
other groups in a time-bound manner.                                              (Para 6.19) 
 
 The Committee is not inclined to agree with the Department’s view. The 
Committee is aware that as per media repo rts during recent time some very prominent 
doctors of the Institute have left it citing pathetic working conditions. This coupled 
with the existing vacancies does not present a rosy picture of the Institute.  
                                        (Para 6.21) 

 151
 The Committee would also like to invite the attention of the Department to its 
concerns raised in its 27th Report on Demands for Grants of the Department wherein it 
has cited a WHO report  according to which out of 428 students of AIIMS (studying 
between 1989 and 2000), 233 (54%) are residing abroad. This presents a very disturbing 
picture that on the one hand there is acute sh ortage of staff in the Institute and on the 
other hand pass outs from this one of the best training grounds in India are turning to 
greener pastures, that too after availing highly subsidized quality education. The 
Committee therefore reiterates it’s earlier stand that the Department should seriously 
come up with some kind of bond or legislation in respect of premier institutes like 
AIIMS so that the young graduates or post graduates are made to serve in AIIMS or 
other Government Hospitals/Institutes for a certain period. The Committee wishes to 
be informed of some concrete steps in this regard.                      (Para 6.22)
 
 
VII POST GRADUATE INSTITUTE OF MEDICAL EDUCATION & RESEARCH 
(PGIMER), CHANDIGARH 
 
 The Committee is happy to note that durin g the first three years  of the Eleventh 
Plan, there has not been a reduction in the Pl an funds at the RE stage. This is an 
indication that the allocated funds are being fully utilized by the Institute for its 
developmental activities.                                        (Para 7.3) 
 The Committee is constrained to note that the fund allocation during the first 
four years (including the current fiscal) amounts to less than half of the 11
th Plan 
allocation.  The funds allocated for the first 3 years of the Plan have been utilized 
optimally by the Institute.  The Committ ee is not aware about any new projects 
proposed to be taken up during the remaining period of the Plan. If that is not the 
case, the Committee can only conclude that th ere is some unrealistic assessment of the 
requirements of the Institute for the current Plan period leading to excess allocation of 
funds.  It is highly impractical to make out that the Institute can spend an enormous 
amount of Rs. 330 crore left for the final year of the Plan. The Committee would like to 
be updated in this regard.                                          (Para 7.5) 
 
 The Committee notes that all the projects undertaken by the Institute during the 
Ninth and Tenth Plan are functional. The only exception  is the Advanced Trauma 

 152
Centre (Phase-I) which is stated to be complete but not functional. The Committee 
would like to have the reasons for the abnormal delay in the operationalisation of the 
Centre.                                                   (Para 7.6)  
 
 As regards the projects undertaken during the Eleventh Plan, the Committee 
finds that out of 14 projects, 6 are reported to be complete and functional.  The 
Committee has been informed that the only major project with an estimated 
expenditure of Rs. 35.92 crore is the Advanced Trauma Centre (Phase II) which is 
likely to be completed by September, 2010. The Committee would, however, like to 
point out that the Centre needs to be made functional also at the earliest.  The 
Committee is not aware about the likely date of Advanced Trauma Centre (Phase I) 
becoming functional. For a project completed in the previous Plan remaining non-
functional by the end of next Plan period  is indeed a cause of serious concern.  The 
Committee would appreciate if Advanced Trau ma Centre (Phase I) is made functional 
without any further delay. Simultaneously preparatory exercise for 
sanction/appointment of required staff and procurement of equipment for the 
Advanced Trauma Centre (Phase II) may also be initiated.                      (Para 7.7) 
 
7.9 The Committee is constrained to observe that nearly 50 percent of the vacancies 
are lying vacant at the level of Additional/Associate Professors. The Committee opines 
that the teaching standards at the Institute are being compromised with such a large 
number of vacancies. This situation can not be allowed to remain to persist any 
further. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Department to go for special 
recruitment drives at the earliest to fill up the vacant positions at the Institute. The 
Committee may be apprised of the efforts put in by the Department in this regard.  
                               (Para 7.9) 
 
VIII NORTH EASTERN INDIRA GANDHI  REGIONAL INSTITUTE OF HEALTH 
& MEDICAL SCIENCES (NEIGRIHMS), SHILLONG 
 
The Committee would like to be briefed about the components of ‘further 
development plan’ of the Institute. The Committee would also like to be given an idea 

 153
whether the Institute has become fully operational and all the pending items of 
developmental work have been completed.                                     (Para 8.3) 
 The Committee is very concerned at the continuance of a very large number of  
vacant positions  in the Institute that is supposed to be developed on the pattern of  
AIIMS, New Delhi and PGIMER, Chandigarh and provide  specialized0 health care to 
the deprived regions of North East India . The Committee notes that the efforts of the 
Institute to fill up Group “B” and “C” technical posts have yielded some fruits. But the 
same is not the case w.r.t. other categories of posts.  The Committee wonders as to how 
the Institute can be considered functional when more than one-third of sanctioned 
Faculty posts are lying vacant and out of total strength of 189 posts of SRD/JRD, only 
83 are in position. Both the teaching and healthcare facilities as envisaged are simply 
not there at present in the Institute.  The Committee is of the opinion that manpower 
alongwith the infrastructure is the foremost  important ingredient which is a must for 
smooth functioning of this Institute. The infrastructure having been put in place to a 
satisfactory level needs to be backed with requisite manpower. The Committee 
accordingly recommends to the Department to approach the CAT (Guwahati Bench) or 
an appropriate authority with a sense of urgency in public interest so that the case is 
settled or at least the stay is vacated to fa cilitate an early filling up of the vacancies. 
The Department needs to keep its relentless  efforts to attract manpower by special 
incentives and special recruitment drives. The Committee may be updated on the 
issue.                                           (Para 8.6) 
 
IX CENTRAL INSTITUTE OF PSYCHIATRY, KANKE, RANCHI (CIP, RANCHI) 
 
 The Committee notes with displeasure that the matter of creation of additional 
posts continues to be at a proposal stage ev en after about two years of directions given 
by the Jharkhand High Court. If this is the state of affairs inspite of a High Court 
intervention, the Committee is compelled to  conclude that things are not going to 
show any marked improvement in the near future. The Committee would like to point 
out that the Department has to play a more pro-active role in improving the services in 
a national level premier institute, especially because of High Court intervention. The 
Department must be aware that the area of services where CIP used to hold a 

 154
commanding position in the earlier times is the most neglected area of our present-day 
healthcare.                                                               (Para 9.5) 
 
9.6 As regards the redevelopment plan, insp ite of the proposal being approved way 
back in 2008-09, the progress w.r.t. the redevelopment of the Institute is at snail’s pace. 
The Committee would like to have a status note on the redevelopment plan of the 
Institute inter-alia covering the components of the plan, funds earmarked and spent, 
timeline set for completion alongwith the progress made so far. The Committee 
recommends to the Department to give all requisite support – in monetary terms as 
well as in terms of expeditious bureaucrati c clearances so that the redevelopment of 
the institute is achieved in a time bound manner.                         (Para 9.6) 
 
X LADY HARDINGE MEDICAL COLLEGE AND SMT. SUCHETA KRIPALANI

rd March, 2010 informed

Affairs in the beginning of the last year . The approved plans were subsequently 
submitted to specific authorities of the De lhi Government and other institutions and

Forest Department was yet to be received. Accordingly, construction work could not be 
started. As soon as all the NOCs are av ailable, tender would be floated for 
construction of buildings under phase -1  of Comprehensive Redevelopment Plan                    
                  (Para 10.4)

of Phase I of Comprehensive Redevelopment Plan of the Lady Hardinge Medical
