The Committee would like to point out that  Performance Audit by CAG covered all 
the 33 States/ UTs with 123 districts cove ring 2369 health Centres.  These 123 
districts were selected from all the States. In each selected district-3 CHCs, 6 PHCs 
and 12 Sub-Centres were audited. The Committee, taking note of CAG findings, in 
these 123 districts, apprehends that position  would be more or less the same in the 
remaining districts of the country.                (Para 3.7) 
 
 The Committee would like to point out that one of the key objectives of 
NRHM is to provide improved health care services through creation of adequate 
number of SCs, PHCs and CHCs. However, this basic goal of NRHM has failed to 
show the desired results. This has been  amply proved in the latest Performance 
Audit of NRHM conducted by the CAG. The Committee has also been expressing 
grave concern regarding lack of adequate rural health infrastructure, especially in 
High Focus States from time to time. Even though NRHM has completed its 5
th year 
since commencement, much remains to be accomplished so far as infrastructure 
development is concerned. With two more ye ars of the Mission period remaining, 
the Committee wonders if the targets to  overcome the shortfall of Health Care 
Infrastructure would be met in the real sense. The Committee, therefore, once again 
urges the Department to quicken the pace of  implementation so that it does not 
remain very far off from the targeted goals.                                                    (Para 3.8)                                   
 
 

 182
RURAL HEALTH FUNCTIONARIES 
The Committee is also quite disappointed with the progress made over the 
past four years regarding filling up the vacant posts of the various healthcare 
functionaries under NRHM. The Committe e observes that one of the most 
important objective with which the Mission was launched at the outset was to 
ensure the availability of the various healthcare functionaries, i.e. ANMs/ MPWs(M) 
/LHVs/ Lab Technicians, doctors and speci alists at Sub-Centres, PHCs and CHCs 
at the right places.  Realizing that thei r presence is essential for the smooth 
functioning of health centres and hence, important for the success of the Mission, 
the Committee has been recommending to the Department year after year to take 
up the issue of non-filling up of the vaca ncies with the States/UTs. However, there 
seems to be no significant improvement in the situation.  The Committee feels that 
four years of focused attention, and cons iderable infusion of funds under NRHM, 
aimed to create a brigade of healthcare is  a long enough time for the situation to 
have improved. Neither the flow of funds  nor the efforts of the Department are 
found wanting. The only conclusion the Committee can draw is that required 
manpower for the Health Centres is simply not available in majority of the States.                                      
(Para 3.18) 
 
 The Committee also notes with dismay that the latest data about healthcare 
functionaries is not available with the Department.  The Committee would like to 
emphasize  that building up of capacity at the grass roots level is an essential part of 
a proper planning process and improvements in this regard can only be noticed if 
the planned measures are put to implementation. One must not forget that these key 
functionaries are also pivotal in the collec tion and dissemination of health related 
information from the field levels. The Comm ittee would, therefore, appreciate if 
updated data is got collected by the Department in coordination with the States/ 
UTs. Only then it would be possible to  make a proper assessment of the ground 
realities and plan for augmentation of both the extant and future capacity of 
manpower in the States.                                                                                   (Para 3.19) 
 
 
3.20 Another area of concern for the Committee is the number of contractual 
appointments being made under the Mission to fill up the various vacant posts. The 

 183
Committee observes that out of total 1,53,568 ANMs in-position in health centres 
across the country, 46,315 ANMs are on contract basis. The Committee is 
constrained to note that such ANMS are co ncentrated in a few states as indicated 
below:- 
States ANMs in-position Contractual ANMs 
Bihar 9127 5896 
Jharkhand 5011 3204 
Assam 8289 4575 
Andhra Pradesh 12541 9505 
Haryana 2592 2465 
Maharashtra 12027 6250 
 
Similarly, out of 4279 Specialists in-position, as many as 2426 are on contract basis.  
(Para 3.20) 
 
The Committee feels that for effective delivery of public health services, it is 
essential that service personnel are present continuously in their place of postings. 
However, with more and more number of  contractual manpower being recruited 
under NRHM, the Committee fears that the accountability mechanism under the 
programme would get diluted, thereby affect ing the delivery of quality services to 
the masses. The Committee, accordingly, recommends to the Department that an 
All India level drive for filling up of the vacant posts, first against the sanctioned 
strength and thereafter against the required strength needs to be  undertaken in a 
mission mode so that by the end of NRHM significant improvement is more than 
evident.                                                                                                              (Para 3.21) 
 
IV       MISSION FLEXIBLE POOL 
 
 The Committee is anguished to note the huge shortfall in utilization of the 
budgeted funds and also the substantial variation between the projected demand for 
funds and the sanctioned provisions.  The Committee observes that at a time when 
equitable access to health facilities across the social and geographical expanse of the 
country is the declared policy of the Govern ment such inefficiencies in utilization of 
funds can prove to be a potent deterrent. The Committee would like the Department 

 184
to impress upon all concerned including the State Governments the imperative need 
to review their existing mechanism for assessment of requirement of funds and take 
corrective action to overcome the deficiencies in the existing system so that the 
possibility of surrendering unspent/under-utilized funds is obviated.                                                              
(Para 4.4) 
Upgradation of PHCs/ CHCs 
 
 The Committee observes that while Tamil Nadu has been able to 
operationalise all its PHCs 24x7, no other state has been able to achieve such a feat. 
The Committee observes that the status of PHCs running 24x7 in some of the States 
need to be revisited. States indicated above  need to double up their efforts so as to 
achieve the targets of making all the PHCs operational 24x7. The Committee fails to 
understand as to what could be the hindrance, specially when funds are available 
for the purpose. The Committee recommends the Department to take up the matter 
of increasing the number of PHCs operating  24x7 as well as upgradation of PHCs/ 
CHCs to FRUs with all the States/ UTs so that a critical service like Health Care is 
available to the targeted beneficiaries wherever required.                            (Para 4.6) 
 
 
Another initiative under NRHM was to upgrade all CHCs to IPHS standards 
by 2010 in a phased manner in all the States/UTs. From the latest information 
furnished to it the Committee finds that in States/ UTs like Bihar, Chattisgarh, 
Himachal Pradesh, Jharkhand, Sikkim, Andhra Pradesh, Chandigarh, Dadra & 
Nagar Haveli, Lakshawdeep, Delhi, inspite of CHCs being selected for upgradation 
to IPHS, no progress has been reported so far.            (Para 4.7) 
 
The Committee also notes that the status of percentage of physical 
upgradation of CHCs in States like J&K (26%), Orissa (13%), MP (4%), Tripura 
(33%), Kerala (29%), West Bengal (9 %) and A&N Island (25%) is far from 
satisfactory. The Committee fails to understand as to what could be the inherent 
constraints being faced by these States in upgrading their CHCs inspite of required 
funds being made available by the Centre. The Committee would like to impress 
upon the Department that the very ob jective of NRHM was to provide quality 
health care facilities which were not available earlier and therefore, the Department 
had embarked upon upgrading the facilities into IPHS. With such low level of 

 185
achievements it cannot be said that the St ates are doing well in the progress of the 
work. This is all the more disturbing wh en there is a considerable gap between the 
required and existing CHCs. The Committee is constrained to note that the 
Department is perhaps not persuing the ma tter with the State Government for the 
need to focus their attention so that the targets are met within their prescribed time-
lines. With only 1255 CHCs taken up for renovation so far, the target of upgrading 
all CHCs to IPHS standards by 2010 is likely to remain unaccomplished.                                                      
(Para 4.8) 
 
 The Committee, however, notes that only 22 States/UTs have furnished 
IDHAPs fully, while, even by the end of March 2010, as many as 12 States/UTs are 
yet to furnish the IDHAPs 100 percent. Goa, D&N Haveli and Lakshadweep have 
not been able to submit a single IDHAP, while Rajasthan has been able to furnish 
only 26 out of the 33 IDHAPs. This is the position when timeline to complete the 
submission of IDHAPs by the States/UTs was by the end of 2008. The Committee 
fails to understand what could be the possible reasons for such inordinate delays in 
the submission of IDHAPs.  The Committee would like to emphasise that Integrated 
District Health Action Plans are the very basis for improving the availability of 
health care facilities in a district.  In the absence of a concrete plan of action drawn 
by the districts, proper health care facilit ies for people residing in such districts 
cannot be developed optimally.  The Committee therefore, impresses upon the 
Department to vigorously pursue this issue with concerned States/UTs to furnish the 
same as early as possible.                                    (Para 4.10) 
 
Rogi Kalyan Samitis 
The Committee would emphasis that there is an urgent need for monitoring 
of the functioning of RKS at ground level. Merely obtaining quarterly/ annual 
reports about funds allocated/ utilized will not serve the purpose. A beginning needs 
to be made by taking up follow-up action with all the States/ UTs where 
performance of RKS has not been found satisfactory by CAG.         (Para 4.12) 
 
 
 
 
 

 186
Grants to State Training Institutions 
  
The success of Family Welfare Programme depends to a large extent, upon the 
availability of qualified, trained and dedi cated healthcare functionaries. Training at 
various level is imparted through the network of Training Centres and Schools. Approved 
outlay of Rs. 669.58 crore has been kept under Eleventh Plan for grants to State Training 
Institutions. The Committee notes that out of this allocation, major funding of Rs. 
520.48 crore is meant for basic training for ANMs/ LHVs and Rs. 56.09 crore for 
basic training for MPWs (Male). The Comm ittee notes that out of Rs. 520.48 crore 
kept for training of ANMs/ LHVs, only Rs . 218.04 crore have been released during 
the first three years of the Eleventh Plan. With Rs. 85.18 crore proposed for 2010-
11, more than Rs. 200.00 crore would be left for the last year of the plan period. Not 
only this, during 2008-09 and 2009-10, the allocated funds have remained under-
utilised. The Committee finds that funds meant for basic training for MPWs (Male) 
also could not be put to optimum use during the Eleventh Plan period so far.                  
(Para 4.13) 
 
4.14 While trying to analyse the reasons for such a state of affairs under the most 
crucial component i.e., training of key functionaries positioned at Sub-Centres and PHCs, 
the Committee finds that limited capacity of ANM schools poses problems in availability 
of ANMs. In other words, the lack of training school for ANMs is also indirectly 
responsible for shortage of ANMs. The Committee was given to understand that States 
have the option to use NRHM resources to restart/ open new ANM training schools. The 
Committee also notes that Lady Health Visitors, a promotional post for ANMs, are 
required to undergo 6 months training in LHV training schools. However, due to LHV 
training schools being closed down in many States, required training was not being 
imparted to LHVs. Situation is more grim w ith respect to training of MPWs (Male) as 
States have simply stopped recruitment of MPWs (Male) for many years due to closing 
down of training schools. The Committee is not aware about the reasons for lack of 
interest on the part of State Government s for creating/ strengthening the training 
schools meant for key Health functionaries with the support of Central Funds. The 
Committee strongly feels that it is high  time for the Department to make an 
assessment of this crucial area in consultation with all the States/ UTs and bring 

 187
about all round improvement. One must not forget that a fully trained and qualified 
health worker alone can handle his job responsibilities effectively.       
(Para 4.14)                                 
 
Training of ASHAs 
 
The Committee views with serious concer n the gap in meeting the targets of 
ASHA training upto IV th Module in most of the States. The Committee is not 
convinced by the reason furnished by the Department that the delay is only confined 
to the 4th Module training in ASHAs in some of  the States. Keeping in view the key 
role ASHA plays in achievement of various NRHM objectives, these gaps need to be 
addressed with a sense of urgency. The Committee observes that given the low level 
of availability of health care services in th e rural areas of high focused States, they 
cannot afford to be off-target in such a crucial area as training to ASHA. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends to the Department to impress upon all the 
States/UTs, particularly those lagging behind, to take immediate corrective steps to 
meet the targets fixed in this regard. The Committee would like to be apprised of 
the state-wise updated status of progress made towards bridging the gaps in the   
IV
th Module training of ASHA.                                           (Para 4.18)                                  
 
Drug Kits for ASHAs
  
 
 The Committee genuinely believes that unless all the eligible ASHAs are 
provided with drug kits, the initial health activities/ work bestowed upon her would 
be hampered. The Committee would like the Department to furnish state-wise 
updated details of drug kits distribute d to the ASHAs. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends that along with all the selected ASHAs being fully trained, equipping 
them with drug kits also needs to be monitored. Gaps, wherever noticed, have to be 
removed without any further delay. At the same time the Department should take 
up with all the States where discrepancies have been noticed in the distribution of 
drug kits.                                                                                   (Para 4.21) 
 
Role of ASHA 
 

 188
The Committee notes that from the interim findings of the National Health 
System Resource Centre (NHSRC) note made available to the Committee that 
NHSRC has identified five areas for strengthening the ASHA programme:- 
- Role Clarity 
- Payment mechanism 
- Training strategies and focus 
- Supervision and Support 
- Financing package. 
 
The initial findings of the NHSRC note, among other recommendations, 
confirm the Committee’s apprehensions regarding role clarity of ASHA and 
reinforces the Committee’s view point that ‘a clear set of measurable health 
outcomes on priority goals’  may be determined for ASHAs with a ‘provision of a 
limited range of services’. The Committee understands that by now the NHSRC 
Report on the review of job responsibilities and role clarity of ANM and AWW 
would have been submitted to the Depa rtment. The Committee recommends to the 
Department to take due cognizance of the observations of NHSRC and to take 
immediate measures to implement the same right from the first quarter of 2010-11 
so that positive results in this regards become visible by the year-end. The 
Committee also recommends that in the meanwhile, a minimum amount per month 
as allowance as recommended by the Mission Steering Group, apart from the 
incentives per programme as currently done, may be given to ASHA as 
remuneration.                                                   (Para 4.25) 
 
Mobile Medical Units 
 
4.29 The Committee is not very convinced by the Department’s replies regarding 
procurement and provision of MMUs. The Department has informed that States, 
based on their need and the resources pu t forth their demands for MMUs in the 
state PIPs. However, the Committee feels th at the Department should take active 
interest in promoting and providing MMUs to the uncovered/ under-covered 
districts of the country. The Committee  notes that States/ UTs like U.P, 
Chhattisgarh, Himachal Pradesh are some of the States where the health care 
indices are already on slippery ground. However, with not even a single MMU 
available in any of the district in the afo resaid States one can imagine the status of 
accessibility of healthcare services in under-served/ unserved areas. Non-

 189
operationalisation/ inadequate functioning of MMUs aggravated the problem 
further. The Committee observ es that the Department would have to devise other 
means and methods beyond mere pursuance with the States, to impress the need for 
the presence of MMUs at the district level so that some emergency medical services 
can be made available to needy. 
(Para 4.29) 
 Of the 354 MMUs operational in the country, the fact as to how many 
of them are actually functional is unclear. Moreover, it is also unclear as to whether 
any assessment has been made to find out that the MMUs purchased till date 
confirm to the standards. CAG findings in its latest report are quite alarming. The 
Committee notes that MMUs procured in Manipur remained unutilized as of 
October, 2008 for want of technical know-how and Specialist doctors. Similarly, 
MMUs were duly procured by Tripura but remained unutilized. Expression of 
interest was floated in May, 2008 in viting interested NGOs/ Charitable 
Organisations for operationalising the MMUs.                                         (Para 4.30) 
 
The Committee can well imagine that there may be any number of similar 
cases in other States also. In such a scen ario, the Committee strongly feels that the 
Ministry needs to undertake a country-w ide review about functional status of 
MMUs. Thereafter, constant monitoring at regular intervals has to be carried out so 
as to ensure the accessibility of healthcare facilities to the general masses                                                      
(Para 4.31) 
 V. RCH FLEXIBLE POOL 
Another disturbing trend noticed by the Committee was that in respect of 
many States, Central funds released during th e year were higher than the proposed 
allocation. While trying to understand the reasons therefor, the Committee came to 
know that all the States/ UTs were having unspent balances at the beginning of the 
year. As per the details made available to the Committee, as on 01.04.2008, opening 
balance amounting to Rs. 1157.46 crore was available with Assam (Rs. 126.67 
crore), Maharashtra (Rs. 209.69 crore), Orissa (Rs. 60.99 crore), Tamil Nadu (Rs. 
97.73 crore), U.P. (Rs. 192.33 crore) and West Bengal (Rs. 101.59 crore) reporting 
the maximum.                                                                        (Para 5.5) 
 

 190
This clearly shows that States have generally failed to take the full benefit of 
various Central interventions inspite of their having the discretion to spend money 
as per their requirements. The Committee, ther efore, strongly feels that if despite 
the availability of funds the states are unable  to utilize them, and thus is a cause for 
a  thorough review of strategies. The low utilization of funds has seriously hampered 
Government’s initiatives for bringing down IMR, MMR and TFR. Seriously 
concerned with this state of affair s, the Committee recommends that the 
Department, in consultation with the St ates/ UTs, should devise alternative 
strategies so that the funds allocated are utilized optimally so that the poor and 
weaker sections of the society are not b ereft of the benefits of various on-going 
health care initiatives.                                                                                         (Para 5.6)                                  
The Committee desires to know whet her the PIPs of the States under 
reference has started reflecting the deficient areas as flagged off by the Department, 
and asking for funds to overcome them.                                                          (Para 5.9)                                   
 
ROUTINE IMMUNIZATION 
 
 The Committee is constrained to observe that this is yet another case of inept 
handling of budgeted funds by the Department. The Committee finds to its dismay 
that despite the efficacy and relevance of  Pentavalent Vaccine being in question, a 
substantive amount of Rs. 205.30 crore was allocated for the same out of which Rs. 
184.14 crore remained unutilised and had to be surrendered. The Committee would 
like the Department to exercise greater ca ution in future and not allow repeat of 
such fiscal imprudence.        (Para 5.13) 
  
From a perusal of the information made available, the Committee would like 
to state here that significant progress un der the Immunisation Programme has not 
been noticed in many of the States. This fa ct holds true both for the high focus and 
EAG States and also the non-high focus States .  In some instances like that of Tamil 
Nadu and Dadra & Nagar Haveli, the immu nization coverage has infact dropped 
below the DLHS II rate. The Committee observes that a low coverage rate in some 
of the very densely populated States like Bihar and Uttar Pradesh means that a very 
large number of children remain exposed to the vagaries of diseases resulting in 
high incidence of IMR in the country. The Committee observes that unless proper 
execution of the plans are made by the programme authorities, visible impact on the 

 191
immunization coverage would remain a distant dream. It, therefore, urges the 
Department to take up the issue with th e respective State Mission Directors during 
the next Common Review Mission, who need to be impressed upon to achieve the 
required quarterly/ annual targets.                                                                 (Para 5.16)                                  
 
Cold chain maintenance is the most  crucial component for effective 
implementation of the Immunisation  Programme. The Committee, however, 
observes that so far this has proved to be the weakest link under the programme. 
Committee’s apprehensions have been confirmed by the assessment of cold chain 
maintenance arrangement made under the Performance Audit of CAG. As per the 
CAG Audit, none of the cold chain equipment was available in any of the 124 test 
checked PHCs in Haryana, Meghalaya, Madhya Pradesh, Puducherry and West 
Bengal. Besides, in Bihar, Lakshadweep and Uttar Pradesh, 31 to 68 per cent of cold 
chain equipment was found non-functional.                                                 (Para 5.17) 
 
 The Committee is more disturbed by the fact that Government of India 
budgetary support for maintenance of cold chain equipments, supply of spare parts, 
provision of alternate power supply has prove d to be of no avail. The States have 
simply confirmed their inability to abso rb the funds for envisaged purposes. The 
Committee is of the view that the absence of cold chain management has indirectly 
resulted in adverse impact on the UIP, a high priority area under the Mission.                                             
(Para 5.18) 
PULSE POLIO PROGRAMME 
 
The Committee is happy to note that the Department has been diligently 
pressing forward with various measures to contain the spread of WPV-1 and WPV-
3 types, especially entrenched in Biha r and Uttar Pradesh. The Committee hopes 
that the introduction of the bivalent vaccine (P1& P3) during 2010 would go a long 
way in eradicating polio from the country.  Though the Department has cited that 
the absolute numbers of polio cases have been reducing over the years, given the 
massive scale on which the programme is being implemented with NIDs, SNIDs and 
mop-up rounds reported polio cases have shown no significant improvement during 
the past three years. The Committee woul d like to reckon here that as per the 
findings of DLHS III (2007-08) survey, th e all India figures of polio immunization 

 192
stand at 66.2% only, with Bihar (53.1%), UP (44.4%), Madhya Pradesh (55.1%), 
Mehghalaya (46.5%), Jharkhand (64.4%), Ra jasthan (63.9%)  and most of the NE 
States performing less than the all India average. Given the above levels of PPI 
coverage in various states, the Committee would like the Department to explain us 
as to how the goal of zero transmission can be achieved at the earliest.                                                         
(Para 5.23) 
 
JANANI SURAKSHA YOJANA 
The Committee views with serious concern the high level of pregnant women 
registered for institutional delivery not op ting for the same. This clearly indicates 
that various interventions made under JS Y so as to make aware the targeted 
population about the positive impact of institutional delivery both on the mother 
and child have not proved to be very effective. Situation becomes more worrisome 
by the fact that high focus States where need was the most have been showing such a 
trend. The Committee fails to understand the reasons behind ground level 
functionaries not monitoring the trimeste r check-up of the registered pregnant 
women. The Committee would like to point out that the achievement of 47 per cent 
of institutional deliveries (DLHS-III 2007- 08) from 37 percent (DLHS-I  1998-99) 
although indicates a slow but steady increas e, however, one must not forget that 53 
per cent women still continue to remain de prived of the benefits of institutional 
deliveries. The Committee is of the view that the role of ASHA/ ANM under the JSY 
seems to be limited to payment of cash incentives only. Consistent tracking of 
expectant mother for antenatal and post-partum check-up and deliveries needed 
more focused attention. For this, regular monitoring and surprise inspections need 
to be included as an essential component of JSY.                      (Para 5.23) 
 The Committee has also been informed that an Evaluation Study of JSY is 
being conducted by an Expert Group led by the Executive Director, National Health 
Systems Resource Centre (NHSRC) with inter-alia the following objectives: 
 
(d) Procedure for random verification of beneficiaries; 
(e) Fund Flow mechanism for administering JSY scheme and mechanism 
adopted for ensuring timely payment to the beneficiaries; and 
(f) Impact of JSY services on maternal mortality ratio and infant mortality rate. 
 

 193
The Committee would like to be informed ab out the latest status  in this regard. 
                                            (Para 5.28) 
 
The Committee observes that the goal of reducing MMR to 100 per lakh like 
births by 2012 is very difficult to achieve at the present rate of decline. The goal can 
be achieved only if there is rapid expa nsion of Skilled Birth Attendants and 
Emergency Obstetric Care (EmOC) . It is in this context th at deficiencies in EmOC 
and Skilled Birth Attendance are very worrisome.                    (Para 5.32)                                  
 
The Committee is of the view that in the absence of required ground-level 
health functionaries who have to be qual ified, trained and semi-skilled personnel, 
the outreach of JSY and ot her specific interventions wi ll remain confined to the 
barest minimum. Number of ambitious schemes with high objectives and 
availability of massive funding will not succeed in reaching the targeted 
beneficiaries. Close monitori ng, not on paper but at the ground level, in the real 
sense alone can strengthen the effective implementation of all the initiatives taken so 
far.                                                                                                                      (Para 5.33) 
 
VI. NATIONAL VECTOR BORNE DISEASE CONTROL PROGRAMME                                  
(NVBDCP) 
 
 
The Committee notes that against an allocation of Rs. 442.00 crore in 2009-
10, expenditure reported as on 28.02. 2010 was only Rs. 278.61 crore. The 
Committee observes that this trend of under-utilisation of allocated fund is also 
there in the first two years of the Eleventh  Plan. Against the provision of Rs. 399.50 
crore and Rs. 472.25 crore in 2007-08 a nd 2008-09, expenditure was only Rs. 383.66 
crore and Rs.  299.38 crore respectively. Th e Committee is constrained to observe 
that against the total Plan outlay of Rs. 3190.00 crore for the Eleventh Plan, less 
than half of the funds were released and that too remained under-utilised. 
Committee’s repeated attempts for optimum utilization of allocated funds under 
NVBDCP has failed to make any impact.                      (Para 6.3)                                   
 
The Committee has always been responsive to the genuine demands of the 
Department. The Committee would, however, also like the Department to focus on 

 194
better utilization of the scarce resource s. The Committee, once again, recommends 
that the Department need to focus on analyzing the reasons for reduced utilization 
of the allocated funds and take corrective action accordingly.  
 
The Committee would also like to know whether any commitment charges 
are to be paid by the Department in case of shortfall in utilization of EAP 
component and if so, the charges paid during the last three years.               (Para 6.4)                                   
 
MALARIA 
 
The Committee notes that the number of cases reported is already on the rise 
in many of the States, especially the No rth-Eastern States, where the problem is 
acute. Under the GFATM project all the 77 district in 7 NE States, 7 districts of 
Jharkhand, 6 districts of West Bengal and 16  districts of Orissa are covered.  The 
Committee is not fully convinced by the Departments, contention that due to remote 
and inaccessible areas with poor access to malaria diagnosis and treatment facilities, 
NE states continue to remain a major cha llenge.  The Committee would like to state 
here that the programme has been these for considerable period and with 100 
percent central funding and also Internatio nal assistance, these challenges, should 
have been significantly lessened. The Comm ittee also finds that major interventions 
like introduction of Rapid diagnostic kits , for the field workers, introduction of 
specialized therapy for Pf cases and long lasting insecticidal nets have all been made 
for North East States.  However, inspite of such huge funding under the programme 
significant progress is not visible. The Committee can only conclude that 
supervision and monitoring, which is one of the main strategies adopted for effective 
control of malaria, is perhaps not being implemented to the desired levels, especially 
in NE region and Orissa.                                  (Para 6.7) 
 
The Committee would however, like to draw the attention of the Department 
to the status of some key functionaries under Malaria control programme. The 
Committee is astonished to find that inspit e of special attention being given to the 
NE States important functionaries are simply not there as indicated below: 
- Vector borne Disease Control (DVBOC) Consultant is not there in any of 
the                 NE States and Sikkim.  

 195
- Only 522 PHC-MOs are posted in Arunachal Pradesh. Remaining States 
do not have this functionary. 
- Assam does not have a Lab-Technician/ Lab-Technician(Contractual)  
- Both Assam and Nagaland do not have a Health Supervisor (M) 
- No Health Supervisor (F) is post ed in Arunachal Pradesh, Assam, 
Nagaland, Sikkim and Tripura. 
- Multi-Purpose Worker (M) is not there in Arunachal Pradesh and Assam. 
- Only Nagaland has 28 Malaria Inspectors 
- Surveillance Staff (307) is there only in Meghalaya. 
- Only Meghalaya has 54 Spray Squads. 
- In Sikkim and Tripura Malaria Tec hnical Supervisors (Contractual) are 
not  there  
- n Arunachal Pradesh and Assam there are no regular MPW (M) at all  
(Para 6.9) 
 
 The fact that these functionaries play a vital role in disease detection and 
surveillance cannot be set aside. By not appointing these functionaries, the States 
leave a large gap in the field-level disease surveillance and treatment. The 
Committee can only conclude from the above that it would be impractical to expect 
noticeable improvements in malaria control with key functionaries missing, that too, 
in the malaria endemic States.   Hence, the Committee advises the Department that, 
apart from allocating sufficient funds to these States/ UTs, it should also pursue 
with State Governments to fill up the vacancies without delay so that  the targets set 
to control malaria could be met within a realistic time-frame. The Committee would 
like the Department to furnish details of various measures adopted by the States 
Governments in the past year to fill up these vacancies.                               (Para 6.10)                        
 
KALA-AZAR 
 
 The Committee observes that though the National Health Policy (2002) had 
set the goal of elimination of Kala-azar by 2010, the above target is yet to be 
realized. This is evident from the fact th at 2534 reported cases of Kala-azar from 
the States of Bihar, West Bengal and Utta r Pradesh have been reported during the 
first two months of the year 2010. It is in  this context that two rounds of door-to-
door search which were to be undertaken  for elimination of Kala-azar, assume 
added significance. However, the Committee fails to understand as to why Kala-
azar fortnights could not be observed in  the most endemic States of Bihar and 
Jharkhand during the past year.  Inspit e of the Committee’s repeated comments on 
carrying on intensive door-to-door surveill ance and monitoring by the Department 

 196
the situation so far has been far from encouraging.  The Committee would like to be 
enlightened about the reasons behind not undertaking the door-to-door search in 
Bihar, Uttar Pradesh. The Committee recommends that effective surveillance, 
timely treatment and compliance of treatmen t regimens in all PHCs in the affected 
districts need to be continued without any let-up.                                         (Para 6.13)                                  
 
 
 The Committee would like the Department to apprise it in clear terms the 
number of PHCs in the endemic districts that have been provided with anti-Kala 
azar drugs, the number of household/ population covered district-wise under the 
door-to-door surveillance activities, and the physical outcomes of such initiatives. 
The Committee would also like to be  apprised about the number of NGOs/ 
FBOs/PRIs involved in Kala-azar eliminatio n activities in the above States, the 
funds provided to them during the last th ree years, the cases treated by them and 
the initiatives taken by them to ensure treatment compliance of the patients.  
(Para 6.14) 
JAPANESE ENCEPHALITIS 
 
 The Committee is dismayed to note that  more than four years have elapsed 
since the vaccination programme was launched in 2006, yet the all India coverage of 
JE vaccination campaign is only 46.62% uptill 2009 as against the targeted 85% 
coverage. What is more worrisome is that JE vaccination coverage in four districts 
of Andhra Pradesh, one district of Assam and two districts of Tamil Nadu is nil, 
which paints a very grim picture, especially  in the context of there being no specific 
anti-viral drug for JE. The Committee feels that if such gaps are not addressed 
immediately, efforts to contain JE outbreaks will stand jeopardized. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that th e Department should, in coordination 
with the State Government concerned, chal k out an action plan to achieve the 
targeted coverage of JE vaccination and also apprise the Committee of the progress 
made in this regard. Similar efforts for vacc ine coverage of the affected districts of 
Andhra Pradesh, Assam and Tamil Nadu are also required.                       (Para 6.18)                                 
 
The Committee would also like to be apprised of the progress made towards 
establishing sentinel sites for regular surveillance and Apex Referral Laboratories 
for advanced diagnosis, circulation of stan dard guidelines, capacity building and 

 197
intensive IEC campaign and vaccination with single dose under Universal 
Immunization Programme. Keeping in view th e fact that Uttar Pradesh is the most 
endemic State which contributes 80% of JE cases, the Committee recommends that 
the Government must quickly take a decision on conducting a special JE 
vaccination campaign for left out children in the seven most sensitive districts of 
Uttar Pradesh along with the affected di stricts of Andhra Pradesh, Assam and 
Tamil Nadu. The quarterly progress made in this regard may be communicated to 
the Committee.                                                    (Para 6.19)                                     
 
The Committee is happy to note that finally some action to check aggravation of 
non-JE viruses in Eastern UP have been initiated by the Central Government. It can 
only hope that the situation would improve  this year during the outbreak season. 
The Committee hopes that the manpower required to make the sentinel sites, 
referral labs operational and the Diagnostic kits provided free of cost by National 
Institute of Virology (NIV), Pune, are in position.                 (Para 6.21)                                     
 
VII NATIONAL TB CONTROL PROGRAMME  
 
 To tackle HIV/TB co-infection, TB + HIV collaborative activities with NACP, 
initiated in 2001, have been scaled up acr oss the country. The Committee notes that 
intensified TB/HIV package of services have  been started in nine HIV high prevalence 
States (Andhra Pradesh, Goa, Karnataka, Maharashtra, Manipur, Mizoram, Nagaland, 
Pondicherry and Tamil Nadu) in 2008 and has been extended to nine additional States 
(Delhi, Gujarat, Punjab, Rajasthan, Kerala, Assam, West Bengal, Orissa and Chandigarh) 
in 2009-10 with a vision to cover the entire nation by 2012. Monitoring of co-infected 
patients was started from 4
th quarter of 2008, and out of a total of 31047 such cases, 5171 
cases were treated with a 75 per cent success rate. The Committee is happy to note that 
the Department has been pursuing its conc erns diligently to identify and provide 
treatment to HIV/T.B co-infected patients.            (Para 7.2)                                  
  
The Committee observes that the emergence of multi-drug resistant TB has 
increased the severity and magnitude of th e problem in terms of cost, longer and 
difficult treatment regimen. In this contex t, the Committee welc omes the initiative 
of establishing a network of 43 Laboratories for diagnosis and follow-up of MDRTB 

 198
patients. The Committee hopes that such Laboratories will be set up across the 
country in such a way that people from all parts of the country have equity in access 
to such facilities. The Committee would like to have an idea of the location of such 
Laboratories and also of the structure of charges, if any, proposed to levied for 
availing of the facilities therefrom.              (Para 7.4) 
                           
 The Committee observes that it is a matter of serious concern that a huge 
amount to the tune of Rs. 79.19 crore was left unspent with the States, and that too, 
the scare resources allocated to counter one of the deadliest diseases. The 
Committee is not aware about any specific measure to check this negative trend. 
The Committee would, therefore, like th e Department to at least outline the 
roadmap for reducing, if not entirely wiping out, the extent of unspent balances.                                         
(Para 7.6) 
 
 The Committee takes note of the fact that out of a total outlay of Rs. 1156 
crore for the Programme, Rs. 756.00 crore is the credit from World Bank. The 
Committee would like to be enlightened if the credit from World Bank has any 
financial implications by way of debt or commitment charges and if so, the extent of 
financial liability involved so far.                       (Para 7.7) 
 
 
 As per the information furnished by the Department 2524 NGOs, 19202 
Private Practitioners, 268 Medical Colleges and 150 Corporate Hospitals are 
involved under RNTCP. The Committee would like to be apprised of the extent of 
financial assistance extended to the NGOs, the amount utilized, the number of 
pending UCs from them and the amount involved therein.                            (Para 7.8)                                  
 
 
To the Committee’s query regarding the latest status on the new drug for treating 
patient with MDR-TB being developed by M/s Johnson and Johnson, the Department has 
informed that the new molecule (TMC 207) is currently undergoing Phase II clinical 
trials to evaluate its efficacy, safety and tolerability in patients suffering from MDR-TB. 
Tuberculosis Research Centre, Chennai and Al l India Institute of Medical Sciences are 
participating in this trial which is being unde rtaken in 17 countries across the world. The 

 199
details on the cost, affordability, efficacy etc. will be available only after successful 
completion of the clinical trials and once the drug is approved for marketing by the 
regulatory authorities. The Committee would like the Department to furnish the latest 
status on the Phase II clinical trials and its findings.          (Para 7.9) 
 
 The Committee would like the Department to share with it the state-wise 
details of the Survey Reports while submitti ng its action taken note. The Committee  
has been given to understand that a ba seline social assessment study at the 
beginning of the RNTCP II project had b een undertaken to assess some of the 
predisposing factors contributing to the spread of the disease, and that another such 
survey is being planned to be made in 2010-11 which will also include  evaluation of 
the predisposing factors for spread of TB.  The findings of the baseline survey may 
be shared with the Committee.                                                                        (Para 7.11) 
 
 
VIII. NATIONAL LEPROSY ERADICATION PROGRAMME 
 
 While appreciating the steady progress in the elimination of leprosy from the 
endemic states, the Committee would like the Department to closely monitor the 
efficacy of the measures taken and intro duce suitable interven tions for elimination 
disease leprosy in D&N Haveli.                                                 (Para 8.4) 
                  
 Committee’s attention is especially drawn to the utilisation status shown 
against Bihar. The Committee notes that ne arly Rs. 2.67 crore were lying unspent 
with the state uptill March, 2008. However,  inspite of non release of funds during 
2008-09 and 2009-10 to the State, the unspent balances had to be carried forward 
from March, 2008 and could not be exhausted completely during the next two years 
with Rs. 1.34 crore still remaining by     31
st December, 2009. Similarly, in case of 
Orissa, Uttarachal, Jharkhand, Dadra & Nagar Haveli, West Bengal and 
Maharashtra large sums of money have been carried forward unspent from the 
previous year. The Committee feels that given the trend over the past couple of 
years, this year too, substantial amount of funds would lie under utilised/ unutilised.     
                          (Para 8.7) 
 
 

 200
 The Committee is of the considered view that judicious utilization of funds is 
indispensable for sound financial administrati on without which it would be difficult 
for the Department to obtain the targeted results under NLEP. The Committee is 
compelled to conclude that much needs to be done especially in the endemic States 
of Bihar, Jharkand and the UT of Dadra & Nagar Haveli. The Committee, 
therefore, urges the Department to take up the matter of fund utilisation on an 
emphatic note with the State Programme Officers during its quarterly review 
meetings. The Committee would like the De partment to find out the underlying 
causes of underutilization of funds and take corrective action accordingly. 
       (Para 8.8) 
 
The Committee is happy to note that at last such an important scheme under 
NLEP has finally seen the light of day. Ho wever, with more than three years of the 
Eleventh Plan Period having passed, the Co mmittee can only hope that the benefits 
of the scheme would accrue to more number of beneficiaries during the coming 
years. The Committee would like to add a word of caution to the Department 
regarding having an effective monitoring mechanism so as to determine the veracity 
of the beneficiaries from initial stage itself. The Committee would also like to have 
an idea about the number of beneficiaries (State-wise).                  (Para 8.10) 
                                                                                                          
 
IX NATIONAL TRACHOMA AND BLINDNESS CONTROL PROGRAMME 
 
 The Committee fails to understand the reasons for low level of achievements 
in the first three quarters of 2009-10 under the various activities cited above. The 
reasons for such low achievements are only best known to the Department.    
(Para 9.3)                                  
 
 The Committee recommends to the Department to give utmost priority to 
properly evaluate the reasons for these undera chievements so that the weak links in 
the chain of implementation of the initiatives can be id entified. This, the Committee 
believes, would surely result in addressing implementation of various components of 
the programme effectively. The Committee fe els that if the situation continues to 
remain as tardy as now then the goals set by the Department under NPCB by the 
end of Eleventh plan would remain a distant dream.                                   (Para 9.5)
        

 201
The Committee is appalled to note th at cataract operations in such huge 
proportions were allowed to be performed in eye camps between 2005-08, although, 
Government of India has banned eye operat i o n s  i n  c a m p s  o w i n g  t o  h i g h  r a t e  o f  
mishaps in such camps.  The Committee feel s that the issue cannot simply be done 
away with, especially in the light of the findings of the CAG. The Committee fails to 
understand as to how, insp ite of the claims made by the Department regarding 
regular monitoring of compliance to the guidelines, cataract surgeries continued to 
be performed in camps in the aforesaid states. The Committee apprehends that such 
surgeries in Camps must be continuing even at present thereby putting a large 
number of patients to risk. The Committ ee observes that merely circulating the 
guidelines to the State Governments with no means to ensure their strict compliance 
would not serve any purpose. There need s to be a component of surprise  
inspections in the existing monitoring mechanism  This, the Committee believes, 
would go a long way in ensuring compliance to the guidelines and act as a deterrent 
against violations by the defaulting agencies.                      (Para 9.7)
                
 
The Committee would however, like to point out that achievement level still 
r e m a i n s  l e s s  t h a n  t h e  t a r g e t .   W h a t  i s  m o r e  d i s t u r b i n g  i s  t h a t  t h e r e  i s  a n  a c t u a l  
requirement of at least 80,000 to 1,00,000 of corneas per year in the country for 
transplantation purpose.  The Committee is  of the view that besides lack of 
awareness, other major cause for low level of eye donation/collection is that 
infrastructure required for the purpose is  simply not there at the ground level.  
Committee’s apprehensions are supported by the following findings of CAG 
Performance Audit of NRHM (2009-10): 
- No Eye Bank was operational in Andaman & Nicobar Islands, Arunachal 
Pradesh, Daman & Diu, Dadra & Nagar Haveli, Himachal Pradesh, 
Jammu & Kashmir, Lakshadweep, Manipur, Meghalaya and 
Uttarakhand (10 States/UTs) 
 
- As of March, 2008, only 346 Eye Banks were operational in 17 States/UTs 
out of which 97 were in the Government Sector and 249 in the voluntary 
sector. 
 
- a comParative analysis of performan ce in 9 States/UTs showed that the 
voluntary sector collected 76 per cent of the eyes donated. Lack of eye 
donation facilities in Government Hospitals was the reason for low 
presence and performance of Government Sector; and 

 202
 
- almost 52 per cent of eyes collected in the Government sector had been 
either rendered unfit or were used for research as compared to 34 per 
cent in the voluntary sector.      
 
      (Para 9.10) 
 
 
 The Committee is also perturbed to fi nd that there was a big gap between 
overall figures of eyes donated and utilized during 2005-2008 for 9 States/UT 
(Jharkhand, Orissa, UP, Assam, Delhi, Gujarat, Haryana, Tamil Nadu and West 
Bengal).  Out of 13,986 eyes donated under Government Sector, number of eyes 
utilized was only 432.  Similarly, out of 44,964 eyes donated under the Voluntary 
Sector, only 9362 eyes could be utilized.   Notwithstanding the very low percentage 
of eyes donated in the country so far, th e low level of utilization of those eyes 
collected suggests that all is not well with  the existing arrangement and the system 
suffers from some grave inadequacies. The Committee’s apprehensions are 
supported by the findings on the performance of Eye Banks in the following States:

        (Para 9.11) 
 
 The fact that as many as 10 States/UTs  do not have a single operational eye 
bank is difficult for the Committee to accept.  Another point that strikes a chord is 

 203
the number of Eye Banks in the governme nt vis-a-vis the pr ivate sector. The 
Committee notes that the ratio of eye bank s operated in the private sector is 2.5 
times the figure for the government sector.  The NPCB is into the fourth year since 
the launch of NRHM and between 2007-08 to 2009-10, nearly Rs. 650 crores have 
been spent under the programme. The Co mmittee finds that measures like non 
recurring assistance upto Rs. 15 lakh to Eye Banks, recurring assistance of Rs. 1500 
per pair of eyes towards honorarium of Eye  Banks Staff, appointment of Eye 
donation Counsellors have also seemed to have made no impact.        
(Para 9.12)                         
 
 The Committee has been given to understand that it is normal that all cornea 
collected cannot be used for replantation purpose, as it needs to be assessed by 
cornea specialist for suitability and quality of cornea.  Only 30 to 40 percent corneas 
collected are suitable for optical Keratopl asty.  The Committee would like to point 
out that as per the above st ate-specific details, percentage of corneas utilized is 
much below the accepted 30 to 40 percent level.  One obvious reason for such a low 
level of utilization can be the upkeep and maintenance of Eye Banks being not as 
per the standard norms. The Committee , therefore, recommends to the Department 
to keep the activities of all the entities/institutions  involved in providing eye 
collection activities under constant scruti ny/monitoring and take urgent remedial 
steps so that the stumbling blocks in the optimum utilization of eyes collected is 
achieved by more number of beneficiaries getting back their vision.                          
 The Committee would like to know as to whether the Government is aware 
of the work done by Dr. Sinha.                                   (Para 9.13) 
 
X. NATIONAL IODINE DEFICIENCY DISORDERS CONTROL 
PROGRAMME 
 
 
 The Committee is not very happy with  the trends of fund utilization under 
the programme. During 2007-08 and 2008-09 against an allocation of Rs. 25.00 crore 
and Rs. 32.00 crore respectively, expenditur e reported is only Rs. 19.45 crore and 
Rs. 17.95 crore respectively.  The Committee observes that every year there have 
been substantial amount of savings over the allocated funds. The Committee notes 
with disappointment that this neither speaks of good financial management nor 

 204
good programme implementation under the Programme. The Committee once 
again recommends to the Department to devi se measures to overcome the obstacles 
coming in the way of proper implementation of the programme initiatives.   
(Para 10.3)                                  
 
The Committee hopes that the Empowered Programme Committee (EPC) 
approval must have been received by now and also the biding process on the 
technical specifications of salt testin g kits, must have been completed. The 
Committee would like to emphasise that all conceivable efforts may be made so as to 
ensure optimal and judicious allocation of funds in active coordination with state 
authority for timely achievements of the targets during  2010-11.       (Para 10.4) 
 
 The Committee understands from the above  that as many as nine States/ UTs 
have been highly endemic iodine deficien cy. The Committee, however, does not find 
that any focused intervention has been initiated in the above States/ UTs to reduce 
the deficiency rate. Since, the above ob servation is a result of certain sample 
districts surveyed in the above States/ UTs the numbers would be much more if all 
the districts in these States/ UTs are co vered. Therefore, the Committee suggests 
that a special intervention needs to be made in all of the above States/UTs.  
Intensified IEC activities may also be carr ied out covering all the districts so that 
the prevalence rate comes to less than 10. The Committee would like the 
Department to furnish details of the samp le survey report concerning all the States/ 
UTs covered for its perusal.                             (Para 10.6) 
 
XI INTEGRATED DISEASE SURVEILLANCE PROGRAMME 
 The Committee fails to understand as to why clear information has not been 
furnished by the Ministry against the targ ets set by it for 2009-10. The Committee 
observes that concrete data is necessary for proper assessment of the targets set and 
merely stating that  training programme is in progress or connectivity on Broad 
Band is being established without quantify ing the progress made evinces ignorance 
of the most basic principles of appraisa l on the part of the Department.  The 
Committee would like the Department to furnish details of achievement during 
2009-10 regarding the targets mentioned above in the table for 2009-10 while 

 205
furnishing its ATN and also be more circumspect while furnishing the information 
in the Outcome Budget.                         (Para 11.3) 
 
 
 The Committee is happy to note that  the recruitment and training of the 
required manpower under IDSP is proceeding at a positive pace. The Committee 
observes that the presence of trained surveillance manpower are key to the 
prevention and control of diseases/ epidem ic in such a large country as India. 
Hence, the Committee hopes that the targ ets of recruiting and training technical 
consultants for the remaining 400 vacant post would have been completed after 
due approval for restructuring and extension of IDSP were obtained. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends the Departme nt to furnish the details in this 
regard for its perusal. The Committee wo uld also like to be apprised of the 
progress made towards establishing video conferencing at the remaining sites and 
strengthening the remaining priority labs.                           (Para 11.5)
